30 November 1994

Deputy Foreign Ministers Hold Consultations in
Minsk

AU2911162494 Kiev URYADOVYY KURYER in
Ukrainian 26 Nov 94 p 1

{UKRINFORM report: “Consultations in Minsk’’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] On 23 November, in Minsk,
consultations were held at the level of deputy foreign
ministers of Belarus, Russia, Moldova, and Ukraine.

The participants had an intensive exchange of opinions
on the forthcoming CSCE summit in Budapest and
discussed problems of European security, peacekeeping
activity, and so on.

Within the framework of the consultations, Ukraine’s
Deputy Foreign Minister Borys Tarasyuk met with Ulad-
zimir Syanko, Belarusian minister of foreign affairs.

They discussed topical issues of bilateral relations and
regional security.

Baltic Defense Commanders Agree on More
Cooperation

LD2911145794 Tallinn Radio Tallinn Network in
Estonian 1400 GMT 29 Nov 94

{[FBIS Translated Text] Estonia, Latvia, and Lithuania
intend to further military cooperation between them in
the near future and do everything to integrate swiftly
into European military structures. This was stated by the
commanders of the Baltic states’ defense forces after
their working meeting, which took place yesterday and
today in Tallinn. Major General Aleksander Einseln,
commander of the Estonian Defense Forces; Lieutenant
Colonel Juris Dalbins, commander of the Latvian
Armed Forces; and Gen. Jonas Andriskevicius, com-
mander of the Lithuanian Armed Forces, decided to set
up a joint working group to coordinate activity within
the NATO’s Partnership for Peace program in the Baltic
states. The working group will be led by Col. Arvo Sirel,
chief of staff of the Estonian Defense Forces.

During their working meeting, the Baltic defense com-
manders acknowledged with satisfaction that despite
financial constraints, the _ormation and training of their
joint peacekeeping unit, the Baltic Peacekeeping Bat-
talion, is proceeding without any particular difficulties.
Einsein, Dalbins, and Andriskevicius agreed that in the
summer of 1995 the Baltic states will participate in
NATO naval maneuvers and form a joint naval unit for
this purpose.

Defense Ministers To Confer in Moscow

MK2911123094 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA in
Russian 29 Nov 94 p 2

[Report by Nikelay Plotnikov under the “In Brief”
rubric}

INTERREGIONAL AFF AIRS 1

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow—A meeting of the CIS
Defense Ministers Council is scheduled here on 1
December. It has been advised in the CIS Military
Cooperation Coordination Staff press center that the
heads of the military departments will focus on arrange-
ments for arms and military hardware maintenance, and
material and technical support of the peacekeeping oper-
ations in the Georgia-Abkhazia conflict zone. Also, it has
been planned to discuss the possibility of granting wider
powers to the commander of the collective peacekeeping
forces in the Republic of Tajikistan, and a program of
events marking the 50th anniversary of the Soviet peo-
ple’s victory in the Great Patriotic War. The day before
the conference, the staff will hold a scientific-practical
conference on collective security problems of the Com-
monwealth.

ITAR-TASS Carries Press Review for 30 Nov

LD3011091894 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0555
GMT 30 Nov 94

[Press Review”—ITAR-TASS headline]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 30 (ITAR-
TASS)—Today’s Russian newspapers concentrate on the
situation in Chechnya. They carry the appeal by Presi-
dent Boris Yeltsin of November 29 to participants in the
armed conflict, calling on them to cease fire, lay down
arms, disband all armed detachments and to release all
prisoners of war.

Here are some disquieting headlines: ‘Alarm Growing”
(ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA), “Children and Women
Being Evacuated From Groznyy’” (KOMSOMOL-
SKAYA PRAVDA), “Yeltsin Gives 48 Hours to Partic-
ipants in Chechen Conflict To Stop Bloodshed” (KRA-
SNAYA ZVEZDA), “Chechnya: War Continues”
(PRAVDA), “Kremlin Preparing for State of Emergency
in Chechnya. Russia To Start War Against a Part of its
Territory First Time Since 1918-22 Civil War” (NEZA-
VISIMAYA GAZETA).

“Will it be Possible To Stop Bloodshed?” asks the
TRUD newspaper, supplementing its report from the
Chechen Republic whose President Dzhokhar Dudayev
seeks independence from the Russian Federation, with a
snapshot of three Russians captured in Groznyy.

The daily learnt from reliable sources at the Russian
Interior Ministry that over 120 people, declared merce-
naries, were detained in Chechnyz. Not all of them are
Russians.

There are sharpshooters from the Baltics, tankmen,
signallers and grenade-launcher operators from Ukraine,
Byelorussia [Belarus] and other countries of the Com-
monwealth of Independent States. No more than 20
people are from Russia.

The NOVAYA YEZHEDNEVNAYA GAZETA daily
features an interview with Chechen President Dudayev.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

“Despite everything, Yeltsin remains the most demo-
cratic man for us. All these developments come not from
him but from the ambitions of his retinue,” Dudayev
claimed.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA features the article “Epoch
of Renaissance Will Come to us” by Russian Vice-
Premier Sergey Shakhray. “‘All Russians and our entire
society badly need a new state-wide idea,” he writes.

“The matter in question is not another version of a
narrow-party doctrine elevated to the level of state will.
We badly need another thing: A supra-class philosophy
of statehood coming from the depths of historical and
cultural memory of Russian people, a philosophy of
closely-knit and single community of a multi-national
people on Russian soil.”

TRUD calls unemployment a new shock for the country,
consequences of which become more chilling. A report
by the Russian Statistics Committce, just published,
does not placate concern: Taking into account people
working part-time, the total number of jobless amounted
to 9.7 million people by November 1, or 13 per cent of
gainfully employed population.

Russia-NATO Partnership Program ‘Virtually
Ready’

LD3011093494 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0812
GMT 30 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Aleksandr Mineyev]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Brussels November 30 TASS—
A partnership progr:mme between Russia and NATO is
virtually ready, and Russian Ambassador to Brussels
Vitaliy Churkin and the NATO leadership plan on
Wednesday [30 November] to agree it up to the end,
ITAR-TASS learnt from diplomatic sources today.

The Russian side conveyed its considerations on the
programme to NATO Council members on Tuesday.

The sides discussed its both components: an individual
programme for military cooperation in the framework
“Partnership for Peace” and a component overstepping
this framework which is characteristic only of relations
between NATO and Russia and which takes into account
its weight as a great nuclear power.

The two components are called the Russia-NATO pro-
gramme in full.

The second component defines the main areas and
methods of mutual information, consultations and coop-
eration. In other words, this is the very “detailed dia-
logue” on which Russia insisted before its accession to
NATO’s “Partnership” programme and consultations
under the “16 plus 1” scheme when a Russian delegate
will be invited to some meetings of the NATO Council.

The Russian side is especially interested in Partnership
cooperation with NATO on such matters as nuclear
non-proliferation, nuclear arms destruction, settlement
of regional conflicts, peace- keeping operations and the
most common issues of European security.

Provisions of the second document of the programme
ensue from a protocol adopted by the NATO leadership
and Russian Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev in June.

The diplomatic sources believe that if the programme is
agreed on Wednesday, the sides will have to settle rather
difficult technical questions of its “launching”.

The programme of events in Brussels for December 2 is
very tense, and it is very difficult to find time for an
appropriate act which is to be attended by Kozyrev and
the NATO leaders.

[By unidentified ITAR-TASS diplomatic correspondent]

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 29 Nov—Russia and

Kozyrev, head of Russian diplomacy, and his Canadian
colleague Andre Ouellet stated this after talks, which
they held today in Moscow.

Both ministers assessed positively the recent statement
by U.S. Secretary of State Warren Christopher on the
post-war setup in Bosnia, which for the first time would
allow a confederative union of Bosnian Serbs with the
union [Federal] Republic of Yugoslavia.

I am very glad and hope that the statement by Christo-
pher is a sign that we are finally beginning to work on a
political settlement, Kozyrev said. In his opinion, it is a
step toward recognizing the right of the Serbs to form a
confederation, a right already granted to the Muslims
and Croats.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Ouellet noted in his turn that Canada was ready to
maintain the presence of its peace-keeping contingent in
the region on the condition that the conflicting sides
moved toward accord. He said that members of the
contact group needed to improve the latest peace plan to
ensure that it was acceptable to all sides, in particular to
the Serbs. For this it should contain “certain stimuli.” It
is important to find new elements in order to move
forward in settling the situation, the Canadian minister
pointed out. A military solution to the problem is
impermissible. Everything possible must be done to
reach peace in Bosnia.

Zotov: Contact Group Plans No Changes in
Bosnia Policy

LD2911201694 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 2008
GMT 29 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Andrey Naryshkin]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Zagreb November 29 TASS—
The international Contact Group does not plan to
change its stand on Bosnian settlement, special represen-
tative of the Russian president Aleksandr Zotov told
ITAR-TASS.

“There is the plan of the Contact Group that aims to
settle situation in Bosnia and Herzegovina, and achieve
normalization throughout former Yugoslavia. That is
what we are concentrating on. Our task is to make
convincing preparations to enable the Contact Group
announce its intentions at the ministerial meeting in
Brussels. These intentions include progress from uncon-
trollable situation with violence outbursts towards stabi-
lization via cease- fire (and termination of hostilities)
and negotiations. Everything should happen simulta-
neously,” said the diplomat upon his arrival in Zagreb
from Sarajevo.

Zotov hopes for “success of the Brussels meeting” at the
level of foreign ministers from states-members of the
international Contact Group to be held on Friday [2
December].

Asked whether Russia offers a solution to the Bihac
problem within the framework of the Contact Group,
Zotov said “‘we are discussing this problem but do not
want to concentrate on local situation, since the Contact
Group should tackle broader issues. Bihac is a pitiful
example of possible consequences of combat operations
held by the sides”.

The presidential representative described the Bosnian
situation as “absolutely abnormal” and said that the
Contact Group called on the parties to the conflict today
in Sarajevo to conclude a cease- fire agreement and start
serious negotiations. “There is no way to settle the
problem on combat grounds, it must be reached at
negotiations,” stressed Zotov.

Abkhaz Declaration of Sovereignty Has ‘No Legal
Force’

LD2811175394 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1601
GMT 28 Nov 94

[By diplomatic correspondents Aleksandr Korzun, Igor
Porshnev, Yevgeniy Terekhov and others; from the
“Diplomatic Panorama” feature]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow will continue to duly
respect Georgia’s territorial integrity, a senior member
of Russia’s Foreign Ministry told Interfax on Monday
[28 November] in comments on the constitution
adopted by Abkhazia’s parliament on Saturday. The new
constitution proclaims Abkhazia “‘a sovereign state ruled
by law.”

The Russian diplomat qualified the step taken by Abk-
hazia’s parliament as “one-sided and having no legal
force.” “The status of independent state is determined
by the will of the people, not by the declaration of a few
dozen MPs. An issue of such scope and significance must
be decided by a national referendum,” he pointed out.

According to the Foreign Ministry official, “‘any state-
ments by the parliamentarians proclaiming Abkhazia a
sovereign state will remain just a waste of breath till the
U.N. recognizes the new status of their republic.” “As for
the world community’s documents, they confirm Geor-
gia’s territorial integrity,” he stressed.

Moscow, the diplomat said, intends to “carefully
peruse” the constitution adopted by Abkhazia’s parlia-
mcit with a view to evaluating the document in legal
terms officially.

However it is already clear that the parliament’s decision
does nothing to promote and step up the normal course
of the talks on sett ing the conflict between Georgia and
Abkhazia, or bring Abkhazia nearer to the acquisition of
the status of internationally recognized sovereign state,“
the Russian diplomat said.

The Georgian leader Eduard Shevardnadze qualified the
proclamation of Abkhazia as « sovereign state by the
Abkhaz parliament as “‘an attempt of separatists to form
an independent state.” This is announced in a statement
released by his press service in Tbilisi Sunday evening.

“Georgia will not conduct talks with the Abkhazian side
as with the representatives of an independent state,”
Shevardnadze said in his statement.

The statement points out: Georgia is confident that the
UN Security Council, the CSCE, the heads of CIS states
and above all the Russian Federation will promptly react
to the new “irresponsible and provocative step taken by
separatists and take effective measures, up to sanctions
envisaged by the UN Charter, to constrain the rabid
separatists.”

Summation of Results of Soskovets’ Tokyo Visit

MM2911145594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 29
Nov 94 p 3

[Article by Sergey Agafonov: “The Visit Is Underway.
What More Can We Hope To Get from It?”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Tokyo—The special-purpose
Russian plane landed at Tokyo’s Haneda airport Sunday
[27 November] morning, and the official visit to Japan
by a very large Russian Government delegation headed
by First Vice Premier Oleg Soskovets began.

As is customary in the descriptions of visits like this, the
day turned out unusually and even stunningly cloudless,
giving. the visitors and their hosts a sense of deep
satisfaction. This feeling of vell-being unfortunately did
not develop into anything more substantial—subsequent
events on the program were stunningly modest in terms
of results and the stock ohrase that the visit by an

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

eminent delegation opens a new chapter (page, para-
graph, line, or letter) in bilateral relations did not crop up
in any of the speeches or toasts even involuntarily.

In my view that is unfair—in fact this visit has already
produced a lot. First of all, three documents were signed
after the two hours of intense talks, which were the
reason why Soskovets flew to Japan accompanied by an
army of officials. In the diplomatic ranking of official
documents, admittedly, these agreements were hardly
higher in caliber than the transcript of a conversation but
let us not quibble at that—accords have been reached on
the creation of a bilateral intergovernmental commission
on trade and economic questions, on the continuation of
Japanese advice for the Russian reforms, and on Japa-
nese assistance t> Russia in joining GATT and its
successor, the World Trade Organization.

The first memorandum in the list is especially valu-
able—for Japan the creation of an intergovernmental
commission on trade and economic questions with a
single country is a completely new departure (they have
managed to do without such a structure even in the keen
dialog with the Americans), but for Russia there are two
advantages. First, this is a new platform for talks and
discussions on the important problems of the benefit of
economic ties, which holds out the prospect of new and
frequent visits to Japan for those in the know. Second,
this gives the first vice premier another leading position
in an interesting sector to add to his existing dozen.
Incidentally, in addition to questions of nonpayments
and all the day-to-day issues in the Russian economy,
Oleg Soskovets already heads a similar committee which
specializes in talks on the benefit of economic ties with
France, so he already has the relevant experience and
now only has to find the time for work with the Japanese
too.

I should like to especially emphasize the significance of
the third memorandum—on helping Russia to join
GATT and the World Trade Organization. Only the
stark absence of other documents worthy of the vice
premier’s signature can explain the appearance of this
miracle of protocol although, on the other hand, the
miracle itself is evidence that there are no hopviess
situations—if there is nothing to sign during the next
visit the sides can always draft a memorandum on
Japanese support for Russia’s admission to something
else.

In addition to those important papers, the two countries’
envoys had already exchanged a note on the rescheduling
of Russia’s debt to Japan for this year totalling $280
million. It has come to light that the Japanese twisted
their G-7 colleagues’ arms to secure this rescheduling
back in October, so that Soskovets is only indirectly
associated with the decision adopted in Tokyo. Even so,
the note which has been signed counts as a result of this
visit because the very next day the head of the Russian
delegation cited this positive experience when he asked
the Japanese side to give some advance thought to
rescheduling next year’s debt repayments.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

This pragmatic view of the future undoubtedly had a
great effect on the Japanese, with the result that the
Russian delegation was told at official level that the
outstanding debt—now amounting to $5 billion—is of
the greatest interest to Japanese business in Russia.

There were many interesting and useful meetings at
various levels outside the framework of the documents
which were signed. For example, during the talks with
Japan’s Foreign Minister and Vice Premier Kono, Sos-
kovets complained about the apathy of Japanese busi-
ness circles in the Russian market and called for invest-
ments and the scaling of new heights in trade and
economic relations. In reply the guest was advised to
settle the territorial question, create a favorable climate
for investments, improve Russian legislation and the tax
system, and not despair about Japanese apathy, which
will evaporate when everything else has teen settled.
Even the seemingly surefire idea of signing a bilateral
agreement on the protection of investments met with a
negative Japanese response. Soskovets was told that the
investment protection agreement does not mean the
arrival of investments and that to generate documenta-
tion at this stage does not seem very advisable.

At Soskovets’ meeting with Japanese Prime Minister
Murayama, which was confined to half an hour, the
Russian delegation head handed to the Japanese premier
letters from Yeltsin and Chernomyrdin: The texts were
identical and contained an invitation to visit Russia. The
Japanese premier accepted the invitation and noted that
his visit should produce tangible improvements in polit-
ical and economic matters. In other words, if there is a
shift on the territorial issue everything else will follow
accordingly.

It remains to be noted that the Russian delegation’s
attempts to secure an increase in the credit line from the
Japanese export-import bank did not end in success—
since Tokyo had promised the G-7 that it would give
$400 million it stuck to its guns. However, there is one
problem where the Japanese are prepared to make con-
cessions—the problem of redirecting $500 million prom-
ised long ago for humanitarian purposes. Moscow asked
to be allowed to use part of this money for investment in
social projects. Tokyo does not object in principle but
the discussion on this issue will only begin when the
$400 million from the expert-import bank have been
successfully taken up.

Japan’s Murata Discusses Defense, Other Ties

MM2911151994 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA in
Russian 29 Nov 94 p 3

[Exclusive interview with Naoaki Murata, director gen-
eral of the defense policy department at the Japan
Defense Agency, by Aleksandr Golts; place and date not
given: “Moving Step by Step Along the Path of Cooper-
ation”—first two paragraphs are introduction]

[FBIS Translated Text] Naoaki Murata, director general
of the Japan Defense Agency defense policy department,
has visited Moscow recently. He took part in a tripartite
(Russia, United States, Japan) i \rum on problems of the
Asia-Pacific region, and had a meeting with Colonel
General Vladimir Zhurbenko, first deputy chief of the
Russian Armed Forces General Staff, who, as ITAR-
TASS reported, noted after the talks had ended that
although a difference of opinion remains on settling the
problem of security in the Asia-Pacific Region, the talks
are making it possible to find points of contact.

Mr. Murata is the most high-ranking of all the represen-
tatives of the Japanese military department who have
visited our capital. The only Russian newspaper which
he agreed to give an interview to was KRASNAYA
ZVEZDA.

[Golts] Mr. Murata, the Japanese press is viewing your
visit to Russia as testimony of the fact that the Japan
Defense Agency intends to expand relations with the
Russian Ministry of Defense. In this connection there is
talk of a possible exchange of visits by the leaders of the
defense departments. Do these reports correspond to
reality?

[Murata] First of all, I would like to say that Janan deems
stabilization and an improvement in the situation in the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

8 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Asia-Pacific Region to be extremely important. In the
context of ensuring security in the Asia-Pacific Region,
as far as Russia is concerned, we view this neighboring
country, which possesses enormous military might, as a
great power. And we consider it necessary to develop
contacts in order to strengthen mutual trust and thus
strengthen security in the Asia-Pacific Region. As for the
ties which already exist between us, then an exchange of
information is going on between scientific research insti-
tutes of the Japan Defense Agency and of the Russian
Ministry of Defense. Apart from that, during the Russian
president’s visit to our country, an agreement was signed
on ensuring the safety of navigation. Representatives of
the two countries’ defense departments are participating
in regular consultations between the foreign ministries.

My visit and participation in the tripartite forum also
testify to a desire to expand these contacts. But I have to
say that there is a serious unresolved territorial issue in
our countries’ relations. The Japanese Government
believes that it should be settled as part of a whole, by
harmonizing our relations in all spheres. The Japan
Defense Agency is also taking this into consideration in
its contacts with the Russian side.

As we gradually move forward, the level of our contacts
will also be raised. Ultimately, we should have an
exchange of visits by the heads of the defense depart-
ments. Our agency is displaying great interest in this. In
a recent conversation with the Russian ambassador in
Tokyo, the head of the Japan Defense Agency stated that
he would like to visit Russia at a suitable time.

[Golts] How does the Japan Defense Agency generally
assess the prospects of the development of the strategic
situation in the Asia-Pacific Region, where on the one
hand the dynamic processes of economic and political
integration are continuing, but on the other hand new
threats to security are appearing? How does the Japan
Defense Agency plan to react to these new threats?

[Murata] First of all, | would like to say that both in
terms of their geographic position and in their history,
the Asia-Pacific Region countries differ more among
each other than European countries do. And this makes
settling security problems a very specific task. The
Asia-Pacific Region is experiencing the consequences of
the Cold War to a far greater extent than Europe. Suffice
it to mention the very complex problem of the Korean
peninsula. The troops of the North and South, which
number 1.5 million servicemen in all, are continuing to
oppose each other just like they did before. Serious
problems have recently arisen in connection with the
DPRK’s nuclear and missile programs. In this connec-
tion, the Japanese Government supports the agreement
between the United States and North Korea. At the same
time, we deem it vital to carefully ensure that this
agreement is carried out, and does not remain just a
scrap of paper. By and large, the problem of the prolif-
eration of nuclear weapons, a problem which goes
beyond the framework of regional policy, is a source of
great concern to us.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

I must also mention China. In the economic respect, this
country is pursuing an “open door” policy. However,
this country’s military budget is growing. While the
question of a universal ban on nuclear testing is on the
agenda, th2 PRC is conducting tests. Of course China has
its reasons for this, but we acknowledge all of these cases
with regret. Economic growth in ASEAN countries has
led to a situation where they are seeking to modernize
their armed forces. We would not like this to lead to
destabilization in the situation in Southeast Asia. I must
also mention that very powerful armed forces remain in
the Russian Far East, although they are being reduced at
the present time.

As for the economic integration cf the Asia-Pacific
Region states, then from the point of view of strength-
ening security, this is undoubtedly a positive process. A
forum has now come into being in the region, at which
the leaders of many states in the region have been given
the opportunity to exchange opinions on a broad range
of problems. We believe that dialogue on defense ques-
tions, problems of the nonproliferation of nuclear
weapons, and confidence-buiiding measures will be pos-
sible in the future. In this connection, we consider
cooperation between the United States, China, Japan,
South Korea, and the ASEAN countries to be very
important. This cooperation should lead to the strength-
ening of confidence-building measures and the provision
of the “transparency” of armed forces and military
activity. Here we expect a great deal from Russia. At the
same time, Japan’s defense policy is determined by a
desire to improve the “Self-Defense Forces’ and
strengthen: “dialogue on security” with the United
States.

[Golts] At the moment Russia is not being particularly
successful in integrating itself in the economic system of
the Asia-Pacific Region. A lot of people believe that this
process of integration would proceed more successfully if
Russia, together with other states, were to participate
actively in the creation of a security system in Asia.
What do you think atout this?

[Murata] As I have already said, dialogue on security
problems in Asia is only just beginning. It is hard to
instruct the Asia-Pacific Region states to discuss the idea
of collective security all at once. We must develop all our
efforts very gradually. Of course, the most favorable
pictures of our future cooperation in the sphere of
security may be painted, but we consider it to be more
realistic to gradually accumulate positive experience
and, taking this experience into account, to carry on
advancing step by step.

Yeltsin, Others Seen Entering Flection Fray

MM2911131594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 26
Nov 94 p 4

(“Express Analysis of the Week” by the IZVESTIYA
Analysis Center: “The President Has Not Been Left
Behind”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] This week Russians had the
chance to play the well-known game of “I’m the king of
the castle, you’re the dirty rascal....”” The initialiy chaotic
reshuffle of key figures in the government gave way to a
slick “talking heads” show, asking potential voters to
decide who is to be king of the castle right now and who
is not.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 9

The election suitcases are either being or have already
been packed. Mr Zhirinovskiy has been packed and
ready for some time now and has been working hard to
strengthen the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia’s
regiunal organizations (with cries of ‘“‘Come on,
Kuban!”"—and it seems to have worked). Mr Shakhray
has made a clear move toward his election baggage,
intimidating the country with the threat of reintegration.
Gaydar also considerably soured the mood of a section
of the deputies when he made a statement about the reed
for early State Duma elections in the very near future.
Comrades Romanov and Zyuganov went on counting
and recounting the lists of signatures for an early presi-
dential election. Then all the well-known faces came out
into the open—G. Burbulis, the “president-maker,” who
did the rounds showing his face on television, and
General Sterligov, who reassured us by saying: “There
will be no civil war. There wili be a national liberation
war.” The election train has started to roll.

Until the very last moment there was clearly one figure
missing from this train. By the end of the week this
deficiency was finally removed and it may now be said
that our executive has begun to sense that there are
“elections in the air.”

Election preparations are a laborious business. You have
to clarify the rules of play in the election districts (to your
own advantage, of course). You have to arm yourself
with the necessary instruments for influencing the
voters. You have to “dust off’ your ideological attire.
Close ranks. And provide yourself with “standby air-
fields” in case of emergency. The executive !aunched a
vigorous campaign this week in all the aforementioned
areas.

THE RULES OF PLAY

Clearly, the most important factor influencing the result
of elections is the means of holding elections (the
CPSU’s experience is an example of that—the result of
elections was known long before they began). Probably
that is why one of the first actions in the ruling party’s
election campaign was to try to push through the Duma
the draft laws “On the Election of Russian Federation
State Duma Deputies” and “On a Russian Federation
Referendum,” with which the president tried to amend
the established rules accordingly, specifically by
increasing the number of single-seat districts from 225 to
300 and reducing the quotas of deputies elected from the
party lists from 225 to 150 (entirely in the spirit of A.1.
Solzhenitsyn’s recommendations). The deputies ele-
gantly parried this attack by adopting the draft laws but
with amendments. Most probably the battle in this sector
will go on until a victorious conclusion. Until the disso-
lution of the State Duma, perhaps?

INSTRUMENTS

Television is the most powerful weapon in an election
struggle. It is in that vein that we should regard the
promise to merge the two state TV companies

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

10 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

(Ostankino and RTV). As has become known, this idea
was launched courtesy of the premier himself, who
personally presented the relevant draft to the president
for his signature. Meanwhile, this amalgamated TV
monster was supposed to be headed by someone close to
Chernomyrdin. Let us recall that this is the premier’s
second attempt to get his hands on the small screen—he
made his first attempt a year ago (also before elections,
by the way), when he tried to place “RTV-parliament,”
the TV company which had been wrested from Khasbu-
latov’s grasp, at the government’s disposal. Just as in
1993, the matter ended in confusion—the president
decided not to place this important position at the
premier’s disposal.

IDEOLOGY

Evidently the “ruling party” has realized the need to
change its jargon. The electorate really has grown tired of
such cliches as “the market,” “democracy,” and so forth.
New rhetorical devices have to be introduced. The
president went to border with Estonia to try out the first
of them. There he used the long-forgotten expression
about not surrendering a pood of Russian soil. The
“pood” was transformed into the centimeter—without,
however, dispelling the analogy with Aleksandr Nevskiy.
To complete the association with the past he should have
added: “‘We do not want a pood of anyone else’s soil...”

COHESION OF RANKS

A party is strong only when it is monolithic. But if it is
infected with the virus of factionalism, decisive treat-
ment is needed.

The present executive is no exception in terms of either
the symptoms of the disease or the desire to be cured. A
characteristic example is provided by an article in
ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA on 19 November, which
openly posed the question of disloyalty by a junior
partner (the Moscow authorities) toward a senior partner
(the federal authorities) and issued stern warnings about
the impermissibility of separate actions.

The conclusion may be drawn that the “ruling party, ”
which has so far tolerated a certain willfulness in its
“regional branches,” henceforth wants to consolidate.

A STANDBY FIELD

The strategist who on the eve of a major battle has not
equipped his positions properly is a bad strategist. The
manifestation of that in our case is when certain group-
ings of the “ruling party” begin preparing for the election
battles by starting to create subordinate political struc-
tures at federal level in whose organization they have
undoubtedly taken account of the latest experience of the
activity of the corresponding regional associations (the
Krasnodar “Kuban,” the Samara “Association in Sup-
port of the Reforms” and so forth).

For example, last week it came to light that in December
there is going to be a constituent congress of a party

FBIS-SOV-94-23¢
30 November 1994

theoretically known as ““New Regional Policy,” created
on the basis of the faction of the same name and
consisting primarily of deputies representing the fuel and
energy complex.

A similar structure, known as the “Party of the Major-
ity” (nurtured for a year now by Moscow financiers close
to the capital’s leadership) is now merging with the
remnants of A. Volskiy’s structures into a single electoral
bloc known as “Russian Civic Union-Third Force. The
name itself and the bloc’s first statements reveal the
familiar ideas of a centrism understood as an equidis-
tance from both the ’authorities* and the “opposition*
to those authorities. However, it is highly characteristic
that in addition to the pro-president Independent
Miners’ Union, the Aircrew Association, and a number
of minute parties and organizations which have declared
their intention to join the bloc there is also the so-called
Patriotic Party—V. Shchegortsov, former chief of the
section for work with parties and public movements in
the Presidential Staff, signs himself as the party's leader.

The situation is quite obvious: A senior official with as
yet not entirely defined functions has been installed to
influence the structure.

This bloc, even if it considerably expands and grows, is
hardly going to be used to nominate the main contender
from the “ruling party” (Yeltsin or Chernomyrdin) for
the presidency.

But let us suppose that the electoral situation in 1996
develops unfavorably for the state’s top officials—then
the entire clan of the powers that be will have to gamble
on another (as yet unknown) figure, preferably one who,
formally, is dissociated from the power structures. In
that event the Russian Civic Union could well be used as
the runway from which the election campaign of General
X or Governor Y takes off.

What is the purpose of this patent political activity by
the federal executive in what is obviously the run-up to
elections? This question is hard to answer with any
degree of certainty at the moment. Primarily because the
president’s position is unclear. His pre-election tactics
are only just emerging (the “‘old-new” methods of public
policy, ideological postulates, and slogans are clearly
being tested out for effectiveness). The president's
energy, initiative, and dynamism and his daily schedule
crammed with public events and meetings call to mind
the now-fading memories of the Yeltsin of 1991, who
was bound to win given that the Union authoriites of the
time had disintegrated. Will Yeltsin the Politician get his
full “second breath,” or is this a burst of short-term,
specific activity that will soon give way to a new and
prolonged bout of passivity?

The question cannot be considered purely rhetorical. All
the political forces which have entered themselves for
the political marathon are interested in the earliest
possible answer: No one wants a false start.

Political Declaration To Be Major CSCE Summit
Document

LD2911192794 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1820
GMT 29 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Sergey Zhirnikhin]

{FBIS Transcribed Text] Budapest November 29
TASS—A political declaration will be the major docu-
ment of the CSCE summit in Budapest, head of the
Russian delegation to the current CSCE preparatory
conference and director of the Russian Foreign Minis-
try’s department for European cooperation Yuriy Ush-
akov told an exclusive ITAR-TASS interview today.

It will be a brief document clear for both specialists and
average people to estimate the current European situa-
tion and outline tasks CSCE is facing, he said. Proposals
of the Russian delegation to raise CSCE efficiency and
make CSCE a fully-fledged regional organization with a
juridical basis and rules are being discussed at the CSCE
conference.

The conference has approved of the Russian thesis
saying CSCE will continue to play a central role in
provision of European security and stability. This state-
ment has been included in the draft political declaration
of the Budapest summit. The Russian delegation hopes
that these paragraphs of the major document will remain
as they are.

Russia has also suggested to discuss a comprehensive
security model for Europe of the 21st century, stressed
the Russian representative. Russia thinks that this model
should be elaborated within CSCE framework with par-
ticipation of all the interested sides—Russia, the United
States, Canada, Western and Eastern Europe and the
Commonwealth states. This model should correspond to
the new times without influence spheres, new barriers
and division lines on the continent.

It has become clear that “blocs, including NATO, are no
longer efficient”. This idea has been supported by the
conference participants and included in the draft polit-
ical declaration, said Ushakov.

Some of the preparations have been completed. The
conference has analyzed CSCE activities since the time
of Helsinki-2. Now it is elaborating documents for the
summit. Not a single document has been finalized, noted
the Russian representative.

Chinese Official on Quality of Russian Goods

LD2811134294 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 0636 GMT 28 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Grigoriy Arslanov]

[FBIS Translated Text] Beijing, 28 November —The
Chinese authorities are planning to step up checks on the
quality of Russian goods coming onto the country’s
market through cross-border trade.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 11

“The expansion of cross-border trade is creating niches
for companies and individual traders who set up illegally
to market their inferior goods, which goes against the
interests of consumers”, (?Qiao Zhi), a spokeswoman for
the Chirese state administration for auditing export and
imported goods said. She also called for all Chinese
commercial companies to carry out rigorous testing of
goods coming from the “former USSR”—metzls, equip-
ment, chemical fertilizers, timber, and motor vehicles.
She said the facts showed the need for such checking.
Inspectors in the Chinese province of Heilongjiang,
bordering on the far-eastern districts and regions of the
Russian Federation, have found that 5.9 percent of
goods which came in from Kussia last year, including
steel, were not up to standard, although they bore official
quality certificates.

Furthermore, it is clear from (?Zhi’s) remarks that some
Russian exporters engage in direct deceit, citing one
figure in documents about the quantity of goods, but
delivering significantly fewer goods abroad. For
example, in one case, instead of 90,000 tonnes of steel,
1,000 tonnes less was delivered. The same thing happens
with chemical fertilizers, with the weight of one package
sometimes 10 kilograms less than the figure indicated in
the specifications. (?Qiao Zhi) claims that in a number of
cases, instead of new motor vehicles, Russian firms have
supplied the Chinese with used vehicles, without tools,
and even with faulty engines.

At the same time, in an interview for ‘BUSINESS
WEEKLY’ newspaper, the representative of the Chinese
Administration admitted that there were also “serious
quality problems” with Chinese goods supplied to con-
sumers across the border. There was one reservation. In
her words, the bulk of poor quality goods are supplied by
individual traders on both sides of the border, while
good-quality goods are supplied through official chan-
nels.

“Cross-border trade between China and Russia has good
prospects,” (?Qiao Zhi) said optimistically. She based
her forecast on the fact that the two countries’ economies
supplement one another—China supplies products of
labor intensive sectors of industry; ready-made gar-
ments, textiles, food and domestic appliances, while
Russia supplies China with products of heavy industry
and raw materials.

The present situation with cross-border trade, accompa-
nied by the reduction in the scope of construction in
China and the economic mess in Russia, is causing a
drop in trade across the Russian-Chinese border. For the
first half of this year, for example, the total volume of
cross-border trade between the two countries was 2.2
billion dollars—39 percent down on last year’s level. At
the same time, Chinese exports dropped 48 percent to
700 million dollars, while Russian exports to the PRC
fell by 34 percent to 1.5 billion dollars.

Ukraine To Accept ‘Zero Option’ To Settle
Financial Issue

LD2911175794 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1601
GMT 29 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Ukrainian Prime Minister Vitaliy
Masol stated that Ukraine is ready to accept the so-called
‘zero option’ for settling the problem of the former Soviet
Union’s assets and liabilities.

He told Interfax, after talks with Russian Prime Minister
Viktor Chernomyrdin in Moscow on Monday [28
November], that just a few minor issues remain to be
coordinated.

The ‘zero option’ implies Kiev’s pledge to withdraw its
claims from the former Soviet Union’s assets in return for
Russia servicing the Ukrainian part of the former Soviet
Union’s foreign debt.

To quote Masol, a protocol on the schedule for discussion
and conclusion of the key bilateral documents has been
signed in Moscow. These documents deal with the time-
table for the repayment of the Ukrainian energy, fuel and
commodity supply debt to Russia—climbing to $635 mil-
lion in 1994 the restructuring of Kiev’s debts to Moscow,
and the problems of the Black Sea Fleet.

Earlier, a source in the Russian delegation told Interfax
that the Ukrainian and Russian premiers had instructed
the leaders of the state delegations to sum up the results of
the talks and consultations on all of the above-mentioned
issues, and present a report on draft decisions by
December 20.

This source also said that judging from this schedule,
President Yeltsin’s official visit to Kiev, during which a
comprehensive bilateral treaty is expected to be signed and
cardinal agreements on key bilateral relation problems

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

concluded, is unlikely to take place this year. It is likely to
be put off until the beginning of the next year, he said.

Masol also told Inierfax that the date of President Yeltsin's
visit to Kiev had not been discussed during the talks. It will
be fixed after the sides have drafted bilateral agreements
and a large-scale political treaty, he said.

Russia’s Vice Premier Alexey Bolshakov, who took part in
the talks, said that a contract had been prepared on further
gas supplies to Ukraine in December 1994. He also said
that an agreement was being drafted on gas supplies in
1995.

The protocol on gas supplies for December 1994 is likely to
be signed on Tuesday, he added.

Bolshakov also said that Ukraine as of late has been
making regular payments for gas, in accordance with the
protocol, although Kiev’s debt is still large.

“Russia cannot supply gas to Ukraine for free. I’m con-
vinced, however, that a specific form of payments will be
found,” he said.

“Russia and Ukraine must belong to a single economic
system” said Masol.

Alleged Supply of Ex-Soviet Arms to Balkans

MM2911103294 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 26
Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Yevgeniy Bovkun: “The Comrade Generals Do
Not Care Who Gets Their Weapons in the Former Yugo-
slavia”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Bonn—The institute for the study
of the problems of the former Soviet Union based in
Frankfurt am Main has published data on secret shipments
of Soviet weapons to the Balkans.

Russia’s generals have generously supplied the Serbs with
modern weapons, the specialists of that institute claim,
accusing M. Burlakov, former commander of the Western
Group of Forces, of being the central figure in this busi-
ness.

Some of the shipments were made via Ukraine. In May
this year Serbia acquired 92 T-72 tanks this way and at the
end of September 83 122-mm howitzers. This information
has been corroborated by U.S. intelligence. After the
withdrawal of the troops from Germany many air defense
missiles also “‘went missing” in the Western Group of
Forces. In all, over 400 freightcar loads of military hard-
ware have already left the Western Group’s arsenals for the
Balkans.

U.S. specialists believe that Croatia has illegally received
just as many weapons, often in the guise of “humanitarian
aid.”’ In Maribor, Slovenia, the local security organs have
confiscated illegal stocks of grenades, automatic weapons,
and missiles weighing a total of 150 tonnes, which had

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS.SOV-94-230
30 Nuvember 1994

arrived via Ukraine destined for Bosnia. The client was a
Muslim “charitable” organization, the brokers were Aus-
trian businessmen.

Paul Beaver, a London specialist on the Balkans, believes
that former Yugoslavia now represents the world’s largest
arms market, where not only the CJ 5 countries are trading
but also the West. For example, in 1993 West European
dealers tried to supply substantial stocks of surface to
surface missiles and carbines to the Balkans through Iran.

The Balkans are also a transshipment point in the under-
ground weapons trade. According to U.S. intelligence
information Serbian state-owned firms have been reselling
some of their weapons to the Near East, in exchange for
Iranian oil in particular, which is then taken to Serbia by
ship through Romania.

Military Delegation Visiting Pakistan

LD2811094994 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1448
GMT 27 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Aleksey Kuznetsov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] New Delhi November 27 TASS—
A delegation of the Russian Defense Ministry and air force
command is expected to arrive in Pakistan on Sunday.
This will be the first military visit at such a high level in the
history of Russian-Pakistani relations.

During the talks, the two sides are to consider the possi-
bility of Pakistan’s purchase of Su-27 fighters from Russia,
according to Indian newspapers.

The first announcement about the proposed visit of a
Russian delegation came from Pakistani Defense Minister
Aftab Shahban Mirani. In an interview with Islamabad
newsmen two weeks ago, he said the visit would result in
an agreement on purchase of Russian-made military air-
craft, however declined to give any details.

“Everything will depend on the final price”, Mirani said,
adding that the approximate price of a Russian fighter was
35 min [million] dollars.

This price is much higher than the one paid by Pakistan for
U.S.- made F-16 aircraft. But out of the 100 fighters
purchased from the United States, Islamabad actually
received just about 60 planes. The remaining fighters were
never delivered, since the U.S. Congress applied a legisla-
tive amendment to Pakistan, prohibiting arms supplies to
countries developing nuclear weapons. After years-long
licigation, Washington announced a few days ago that it
was ready to give back 658 min dollars to Pakistan for the
fighters in question.

As an alternative to F-16, Pakistan considered the possi-
bility of purchasing Russian aircarft and French Mirage-
2000 fighters. The French planes were rejected because of
their unreasonable price of 86 min dollars. Over the past
few months, Pakistan has stepped up negotiations with
Moscow. Pakistani experts visited Russia last October and
examined Soviet Union-27 [as received] fighters.

According to some information, the forthoming visit of the
Russian military delegation to Islamabad will not neces-
sarily result in signing some document. It is quite likely
that the two sides will only agree upon a draft agreement,
which is to be signed during the proposed visit to Moscow
by Pakistani Prime Minister Benazir Bhutto, scheduled for
December.

Russia-Pakistan Talks on Arms Sales Viewed

minister, Islamabad expected a high-ranking Russian mis-
sion 27 November to discuss terms and conditions of the
purchase of 32 Su-27 fighter jets, but without avail.

Yesterday, your KOMMERSANT-DAILY correspondent
was assured at the ‘““Rosvooruzheniye” state company and
the Russian Defense Ministry that these claims are ground-
less—not only is Russia not holding any talks with Paki-
stan on the sale of Su-27’s, it has not discussed any arms
supplies at all with it. It cannot be ruled out, however, that
Moscow’s caution in military contacts with Islamabad is
attributable not so much to the fact that the latter does not
yet have the status of “authorized cooperation partner,”
but rather by possible complications in relations with the
United States.

Information on supplying two Su-27 squadrons to Paki-
stan emerged two weeks ago. At the time, neither the
Russian Defense Ministry nor “Rosvooruzheniye” could
either confirm or deny this sensational statement made by
Pakistani Defense Minister Aftab Shaban Mirani. The
absence of comments itself did not mean that there were
no contacts between Russia and Pakistan on the subject at
all; they appear to have been put on hold as a result of
Mirani’s clearly premature statement making the talks
public.

““Rosvooruzheniye,” however, is inclined to see the hulla-
baloo raised in Pakistan over the Su-27 deal in a slightly
different light. As early as the beginning of 1994 Islamabad
reached an agreement on buying 30 F-16’s from the United
States and even paid about 15 percent of their price in
advance. The aircraft were to arrive in Pakistan in
December, but the United States unexpectedly attached a
number of clearly noncommercial conditions to the sale
and froze the money already received from Pakistan. In
particular, Washington evidently decided to exploit the
deal in order to get access to Pakistan’s nuclear facilities
and nuclear program itself, which, to put it mildly, came as
an unpleasant surprise for the latter. It was Islamabad’s
desire to get Washington to comply with the original terms
of the deal that fueled rumors about its intention to buy
Su-27’s instead of F-16’s in the first place, “‘Ros-
vooruzheniye” thinks.

Indeed, Pakistan could hardly have parted so easily with
the nearly $200 million it deposited in a special account in
a U.S. bank. Or. the other hand, Russia is stepping up its
activity on arms markets literally by the day. And more
countries that have not been counted among Russia’s
“traditional” partners are announcing their intention to
buy Russian-made equipment. Indonesia and Thailand
joined the list the other day. Contacts in military-technical

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 Movember 1994

relations with Brazil are becoming increasingly vibrant. At
the same time, Russia has been making increasingly fre-
quent inroads into markets that have until recently been
considered the United States’ exclusive sphere of influ-
ence. Given that, observers do not rule out the possibility
of an early “Russian invasion” of the Pakistani market as
well.

Commentator on Freezing DPRK’s Nuclear

OW2911163694 Moscow Voice of Russia World Service
in Mandarin 1100 GMT 23 Nov 94

[Commentary by station commentator Kondratyev from
the “Current Events and Commenta’y” program]

[FBIS Translated Text] Listeners ana friends:

With the authorization of the UN Security «-"ncil to
oversee the entire process of freezing North Korea’s
nuclear program, a team of International Atomic Energy
Agency [IAEA] experts has arrived in Pyongyang. This
station’s commentator Kondratyev noted:

It has been learned that the principal mission of these
international experts is to inspect the country’s nuclear
program from the military aspect. The North Koreans
themselves have also insisted on such an inspection
recently. Generally speaking, the apprehension over the
building of nuclear weapons by North Korea can soon be
dispelled.

In the meantime, it is of equal importance that turning
North Korea’s nuclear reactors into light water reactors
will prevent the country from building nuclear weapons.
The price of such a project is fairly high—$4 billion by
experts’ estimates. They also estimate that it will take at
least eight years to change the reactors.

An international consortium will be established in
December, as suggested by the United States, Japan, and
South Korea, to freeze all projects under the North Korean
nuclear program. A number of West European countries,
China, and Russia are expected to be invited to join the
consortium. Moscow has on many occasions offered to
help the Democratic People’s Republic of Korea [DPRK]
change its nuclear reactors, but China has not clearly stated
whether it will join the international consortium.

With the establishment of the consortium, the U.S.-DPRK
agreement on the nuclear problem will enter the enforce-
ment stage. A spokesman for the DPRK Foreign Ministry
said a couple of days ago: Pyongyang has taken measures to
fully suspend the operations of all nuclear reactors. He said
the nuclear waste will no longer increase and the existing
waste will be properly stored. All nuclear facilities will be
subject to IAEA supervision without exception.

This should be greeted as a cheerful remark. However,
there is still a long way to go to turn the Korean Peninsula
into a nuclear-free zone. Therefore, Moscow has proposed
an international conference on the most thorny problems
on the peninsula. An international guarantee will be able to
maintain the Korean peninsula's nuclear-free status.

Yeltsin Edict on Presidential Programs Fund

MM2911152594 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Nov 94 First Edition p 4

[Russian Federation Presidential Edict No. 2092, signed
by President Yeltsin and dated 21 November 1994: “On
the Presidential Programs Fund”)

[FBIS Translated Text] With a view to mobilizing
extrabudgetary financial resources to implement presiden-
tial programs, I decree that:

1. The proposal from the Russian Federation Presidential
Staff's Presidential Programs Center and interested finan-
cial and investment institutions to set up a Presidential
Programs Fund (hereinafter the Fund) shall be ap’ »ved.

2. It shall be prescribed that the Fund is a noncommercial
organization and, within the bounds of its prescribed
powers, mobilizes extrabudgetary financial resources to
implement presidential programs.

The Fund is a corporate body and has its own balance
sheet, property (including monies) owned by it with the
right of uperational administration, current and other
bank accounts, and a seal of an established design.

3. In its activity the Fund is guided by the Russian
Federation Constitution and federal laws, the Russian
Federation's internationa! treaties, edicts and directives
from the Russian Federation president, decrees from the
Russian Federation Government, and its own charter.

4. It shall be laid down that the Fund’s main tasks are to:

elaborate and carry out expert assessment and selection of
proposals to implement presidential programs;

provide presidential »rograms with the necessary funds
from extrabudgetary sources and with personnel;

create financial and investment mechanisms to ensure the
elaboration and implementation of presidential programs.

5. It shall be prescribed that, in order to implement its
main tasks, the Fund is entitled to:

accumulate extrabudgetary financial resources to imple-
ment presidential programs;

provide financial funds on a nonrefundable and refund-
able basis for the elaboration and implementation of
presidential programs;

monitor the targeted utilization of the financial resources
allocated by the Fund and, in the event of violations
coming to light, suspend the financing of the corre-
sponding programs;

implement other activity in accordance with Russian Fed-

eration legislation, its charter, and instructions duly
received.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 15

6. It shall be prescribed that the sources for shaping the
Fund’s financial resources are:

resources supplied by Fund participants;

voluntary contributions from vorporations and individ-
uals;

other revenue from Fund activity.

The Fund’s financial resources are used exclusively to
implement the tasks envisaged by Paragraph 4 of this
edict.

7. A Fund Supervisory Council shall be set up consisting of
representatives of Fund participants. It shall be prescribed
that the Fund’s Supervisory Council shall:

¢ Monitor the Fund’s activity;

¢ Appoint and dismiss the executive director of the
Fund and his deputies following representations from
the chairman of the Fund’s Supervisory Council;

e Annul decisions made by the Fund’s executive
director in violation of Russian Federation legislation
and the Fund’s charter;

¢ Ratify the Fund’s structure and staff roster, as well as
labor remuneration for Fund employees.

The decisions of the Fund’s Supervisory Council shall be
adopted by a majority vote of its members and signed by
the chairman of the Fund’s Supervisory Council. The
Russian Federation president shall be appointed
chairman of the Fund’s Supervisory Council and shall be
annually given a written report and verbal submission
[doklad i auditorskoye zaklyucheniye] on the Fund’s
activity. The leaders (deputy leaders) of Russian Feder-
ation Presidential Staff subunits, the Russian Federation
Ministry of Finance, the Russian Federation Ministry of
the Economy, and the Russian Federation Government's
Federal Commission for Securities and the Stock Market
shall be ex-officio members of the Fund’s Supervisory
Council.

8. The Russian Federation Presidential Staff, in conjunc-
tion with interested financial and investment institutions,
shall act under the established procedure as founder of the
Fund and shall ratify its charter in accordance with this
edict within one month.

9. The Russian Federation Presidential Staff's Presiden-
tial Programs Center and the Russian Federation Presi-
dent’s Security Service shall provide assistance in orga-
nizing the Fund’s financial structure, which includes an
investment holding company and an investment bank, as
well as providing information, analytical, and other
support for the activity of the Fund and its subordinate
institutions.

10. This edict comes into force from the day it is signed.

Edict Seen as Ineffective ‘Ploy’

MK2911105094 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA in
Russian 29 Nov 94 p 1

[Report by Igor Korotchenko in the “Opinion” column:
“Boris Yeltsin Beefs Up Counterintelligence. Fighting
Espionage Will Require Not Only Organizational Mea-
sures but Also Financial Resources’’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Boris Yeltsin’s edict on returning
the investigation apparatus to the Federal Counterintel-
ligence Service [FCS] fold (wherefrom it was taken in the
process of reforming the former Security Ministry) has
been received with misgivings in the majority of Western
special service stations operating in Moscow under the
“cover” of diplomatic missions. Many analysts regard
this decision as a move toward tightening up the coun-
terintelligence regime on Russian territory. In the run-up
to the 1996 presidential elections Boris Yeltsin vitally
needs effective achievements along any lines, capable of
boosting his personal popularity rating among various
sections of the electorate.

True, it appears that the president will not win great
laurels in the area of fighting espionage. After the dem-
olition of the KGB in August 1991 and the decentrali-
zation of its major elements, the world leading special
services (the CIA, MI6, Mosad, and others) received
several years of relatively free work in Russia. Presum-
ably this time has been used to create secret networks
with access to Russian state and political structures. It
needs to be pointed out that technical progress in the
West has not bypassed such a delicate sphere as liaison
with agents. Today, recruited Russian secret-bearers
most often do not bury their reports in dead drops but,
using secret portable electronic equipment, dump
encoded information within one or two seconds either
onto embassy antenna systems or specially equipped
diplomatic cars parked in designated places. The imper-
sonal forms of communication used by foreign intelli-
gence services leave domestic counterintelligence very
little chance to arrest spies.

Therefore, if Boris Yeltsin wants loud affairs (like the
“Ames case”’), budget appropriations for the activity of
Russian special services engaged in counterintelligence
and foreign intelligence will need to be increased. After
all, with the collapse of the communist system the
reasons for which some idealists agreed to cooperate
with the KBG or the GRU have disappeared. Now sheer
material interest is the main driving force for potential
traders in secrets.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Realism of Yeltsin’s ‘Ultimatum’ to Chechnya
Questioned

MM 3011103394 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 30
Nov 94 p 2

[Article by Valeriy Vyzhutovich: “Who Needs 48 Hours
To Reflect? Russian President’s Ultimatum to Bellig-
erent Chechnya Prompts Not Only This but Also Other
Questions”’]

{FBIS Translated Text] On 29 November Boris Yeltsin
addressed the participants in the armed conflict in
Chechnya. After stating that “the hope of an indepen-
dent solution to the internal Chechen conflict has been
completely exhausted,” the Russian president put this
ultimatum-like demand: Within 48 hours of the appeal
they must “‘cease fire, lay down their arms, disband all
armed formations, and release all citizens who have been
captured and are being detained by force.” If these
demands have not been fulfilled by the set deadline, a
state of emergency will be introduced on the territory of
Chechnya.

The degree to which this presidential ultimatum is
realistic desperately reflects the level of realism of the
Kremlin’s entire three-year policy on Chechnya. You
have to have at your disposal a sizable staff of advisers
and a wide range of analytical, information, intelligence,
and other state services in order to seriously believe that
a full conciliation of the belligerents is attainable in
Chechnya within 48 hours. With the melancholy of
Pavel Grachev, you have to be “not very interested in
what is happening there” in order to demand “the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 17

restoration of constitutional legality, law and order, and
peace in the Chechen Republic” within 48 hours.

At the time of writing these lines, Groznyy’s official
reaction to Moscow's ultimatum has not yet been
received. But Musa Merzhuyev, the Chechen president’s
spokesman, has already told INTERFAX that Boris
Yeltsin’s appeal to the participants in the armed con-
frontation “will only cause all Chechens to unite” and
will also “inevitably remove from the opposition camp
those people who have blindly followed it.”

It is clear from the appeal that Boris Yeltsin regards as
one of the “sides in the conflict” the Chechen govern-
ment troops defending the legitimate power and the
legitimate president, regardless of whether the Russian
leadership likes this power and this president. I would be
curious to know how the Kremlin would have reacted
during the night of 3-4 October 1993 to a categorical
demand from Bill Clinton, let us say, to Boris Yeltsin on
the one hand and to Aleksandr Rutskoy on the other
hand to disarm their “participants in the armed conflict”
without delay and to go home to bed.

But Chechnya is part of Russia, is it not? Does this mean
that we can do things unceremoniously and severely, but
in our own way? Yes, Chechnya was and still is de jure a
component of the Russian Federation. But for the past
three years Moscow has, as it were, forgotten (not
counting the sluggish talks) about this obvious point and
has de facto recognized the independence of the Chechen
state. Now, whatever people might say, the introduction
of a state of emergency in this republic might be inter-
preted by its leadership as armed Russian intervention.

It is difficult, however, to believe in Russia’s direct
strong-arm intcrference in the Chechen feuding. The
safety fuse of the ultimatum will burn down over the
course of 48 hours and...will most likely be extinguished.
By the creation of some coordinating and conciliatory
council, center, or staff. Because people both there and
here realize that the introduction of a state of emergency
in Chechnya means the start of a major war which could
set the entire North Caucasus on fire and draw dozens of
peoples and ethnic groups into a deadly circle. Moscow is
not at present proposing any other means of untying the
Chechen knot which is swelling with blood. There can be
no doubt that the Kremlin is now feverishly searching for
them. So Boris Yeltsin has, rather, given himself 48
hours to reflect.

Economic & Financial Issues

Economic Conference Prepares for ‘New Year’

MM2911114994 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 29 Nov 94 First Edition pp 1, 3

[Report by Vladimir Kucherenko: “Time To Compare
Views. All-Russia Conference Devoted to Discussion of

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Draft Budget "95 and Government Actior Program for
1995-1997 Held 26 November”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Opening the All-Russian Confer-
ence “Prospects for Economic Reform in Russia,” Boris
Yeltsin noted that its purpose and main tasks were to
“prepare for the new year of developing the Russian
reforms.” As he put it, problems no less difficult than
today’s will emerge in 1995. The ways to solve them
should be mapped out now.

Analyzing the situation in the economy, the Russian
Federation president said that “hitherto it has been in a
state of crisis.” This state is now close to being over-
come. Consequently, opportunities have emerged for
positive changes in the socioeconomic situation in the
country. As he put it, the situation is changing—although
not so noticeably for the majority of Russian citizens.
The president and government can see that, with certain
efforts on their part, the negative trends of recent years
can be overcome.

He went on to say that the government has drawn up a
draft budget for 1995 and a three-year concept for the
development of economic reform, which now constitute
an action program. Russia’s future place in the world
community will depend on how they are implemented.

Consequently, Boris Yeltsin said, “the time has come to
compare views and determine the program of action for
next year.” The president believes that this can only be
done jointly, through the efforts of the legislature and the
executive, taking account of the opinion of the regions,
Federation components, and Russian businesses. The
conference was convened in this connection.

Boris Yeltsin went on to give a wide-ranging description
of the current situation in the country’s economy.
According to him, 1994 was not a simple year for the
economy, but there have been certain improvements
nonetheless. As a positive point the president noted the
“comparatively modest level of inflation.” As he put it,
the dramatic events on the foreign exchanges in October
have not disrupted this trend. “The October surge in
inflation produced a very painful reaction in society.
But, at the same time, it showed that there is no going
back to uncontrollably high inflation,” the president
noted.

This year, Boris Yeltsin said, “the savings of citizens and
enterprises have begun to grow, although, of course, not
everyone’s have.” And this is a major social problem.
When there are real savings, the president believes,
investment will be possible, as will an end to the crisis
and the prerequisites for an economic upturn.

The situation in production remains difficult, according
to the head of state. “Its slump has been very deep
compared with last year.” There is a structural crisis;
high-tech industry is in a complex position; there are
many problems in the social sphere; and mutual
defaulting continues to plague the economy.“

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Nonetheless, 1994, in Boris Yeltsin’s opinion, was not
lost to reform. Its main result was that Russia’s economy
has started to operate according to the laws of the
market. Consequently, the president added, “‘it is time to
leave the period of backtracking and half-baked solu-
tions behind along with this year.” In 1995 “we will go
on the offensive, complete financial stabilization, and
embark on the creation of the prerequisites for restoring
and boosting the economy,” he stated.

[At this point ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA refers readers
to page three of the issue for Premier V. Chernomyrdin’s
main report to the conference; account of this report is
being texted separately]

According to the Federation Council chairman [V.
Shumeyko], the government has been unable to move to
strategic planning for the economy and has failed to
implement the president’s February message to the Fed-
eral Assembly. We have had a purely outline forecast,
whereas the government should have set goals and
achieved them. It is necessary to move toward a planned
economy on an indicative basis.

The Federation Council chairman does not believe that
the 1995 budget should be entirely balanced. Only we
need to direct the money obtained from the “printing
presses” not into current spending—as is the case at
present—but into strategic investment programs, which
will produce huge returns in the future.

V. Shumeyko proposes supporting high-tech and eco-
nomic production facilities in the budget, which would
not only ensure that the country joins the world market,
but would support the integrity of the national economy
and make the country self-sufficient and relatively inde-
pendent of imports. But the policy of the Economics
Ministry (of helping only projects that show a rapid
return) is leading to the loss of scientific and technical
potential.

Budget relations between the center and the provinces
are another unregulated area of dispute. V. Shumeyko is
calling for “budget federalism’—a division of responsi-
bility in implementing reform. The current system of
transfer payments (when the regions pay money to
Moscow, which then redistributes it) is, as the senator
put it, destroying the incentives to boost incomes in the
territories. The dissatisfaction of the oblasts, which want
equal rights with the republics and autonomous areas, is
increasingly vocal. They do not like it when the latter,
sometimes with sparse and smaller populations, pay less
tax but get more aid.

Similar thoughts were voiced by State Duma Speaker I.
Rybkin. The goals set by the government for next year, in
his opinion, coincide with the memorandum that
Gaydar signed for the IMF in 1992 and with the already
adopted action program for 1993-1995. He expressed
doubt about the feasibility of these plans.

Treasury spending should not be reduced without giving
thought to how to increase revenue. And this is unthink-
able without supporting production. “Let’s stop thinking

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 19

in terms of trite cliches!” Rybkin proclaimed. “We
should spend money not on supporting the military-
industrial complex nor on subsidies to the agro-
industrial complex, but on preserving our technological
base and unique collectives, and providing support for
the countryside and even, if you like, for the roots of our
ethnic group, our people!” It is from this standpoint that
criteria ought to be determined, the budget adopted, and
taxes formed. Rybkin recalled that Japan and the United
States have even announced tax holidays for spheres of
the economy which could be the engines to pull the
economy out of crisis. And these were by no means the
services or imported goods spheres. For the sake of a
balanced budget we are literally ruining our producers.

It is also foolish to give up Central Bank credits. Rybkin
recalled that the repayment of our domestic and foreign
debt will fall due at the time of the presidential and
parliamentary elections. This is a very explosive situa-
tion.

Relations with Federation components could be a “time
bomb.” According to Rybkin, it is necessary to set up a
regional justice program, defining areas of future devel-
opment.

Federation components had their own view as well.
President V. Kokov of Kabardino-Balkaria, while
warmly approving the government’s activity, com-
plained about the poor mechanism of interbudgetary
relations between the center and the regions. They are
currently maintaining the backward state of the North
Caucasus. On the other hand, Kokov complained of the
cumbersomeness of the bureaucratic system of running
the country, and the irresponsibility and lack of disci-
pline shown by its cadres. For instance, the presidential
edict issued in October 1992 on support for Kabardino-
Balkaria has been just 20-percent fulfilled.

A. Lisitsyn, head of the Yaroslavl! Oblast Administra-
tion, pointed out that the draft 1995 budget did not
reflect the question of switching the social and consumer
sphere from enterprise budgets—which cannot sustain
it—to municipalities’ budgets. There is a threat of col-
lapse, he warned. The Finance Ministry should fully
hand things over to the regions, leaving them some of the
corresponding taxes for the upkeep of the social, cultural,
and consumer sphere.

The budget also faces the threat of being destroyed by
defaulting. Governors should combat this by carrying
out mutual offsetting of debts and by “issuing” bills of
exchange and loans. But, although all this should be done
simultaneously in all the regions, most of them are in no
hurry to do so.

Governor L. Polezhayev of Omsk believes that the
actions of market mechanisms in trans-Ural areas should
be restricted. He is not happy about the prospects for a
new prices policy and the fact that prices of energy
sources are nearing world levels. With the ruble’s fall
against the dollar this is destroying production and
increasing its costs.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

20 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

The situation in this area is not gratifying either. The
banks have usually served as examples of prosperity
amid the production crisis. But T. Paramonova, acting
chairman of the Central Bank of Russia, has warned that
this sphere too is now in a difficult position for the first
time in the reform period. One-fifth of all commercial
banks will end the year with losses. Some 200 establish-
ments simply have no funds in their correspondent
accounts, and it is planned to take away the licenses of
approximately 100 banks on the grounds that they are
bankrupt.

There are several reasons for this. First and foremost, the
difficult situation facing the banks’ clients—enterprises.
Then the disorganization of the banking sphere itself.
Unlike their Western colleagues, our financiers are
destroying their banks by lending to bad risks. Above all,
to enterprise sponsors. And the Central Bank of Russia
can do nothing about it—a new law “On Banks and
Banking Activity” is required.

The fact that new laws on banking activity and on the
Central Bank of Russia are needed was also mentioned
by V. Vinogradov, head of “Inkombank”—without them
there can be no normal deposit insurance, recapitaliza-
tion of failed banks, or help to those banks which have
been the “cornerstones” of regional economies.

In order to normalize the economic situation V.
Vinogradov is calling for an unchanging and simple tax
system to be established for two or three years, adopting
clear-cut rather than vague decisions at top level. So far
the state is providing poor incentives for private invest-
ment. It is planned that for every four rubles of private
investment the budget will invest one ruble. But,
according to the banker, this is not enough—we need a
50-50 ratio, or even 15-85.

I. Yermakova, president of the “Ruskhim” Financial-
Industrial Group, believes that all investments will fail
unless order and discipline are restored and property
relationships regulated. Unless this is done it would be
better for loss-making enterprises—no matter how many
of them are privatized—to shut down. Thus, 20 percent
of shares in one of the group’s enterprises were acquired
by a commercial structure which stopped paying for the
plant’s products, literally sucking it dry.

The opinion of military-industrial complex entrepre-
neurs was voiced by G. Yefremov, director of the
“‘Mashinostroyeniye” Science and Production Associa-
tion in Reutov: “Russia is giving up its treasure—its
technological base.” Unique production facilities are
being destroyed and teams of people are being broken
up—just who will provide the modern technology that
the country needs to overcome the crisis in restructuring
its production? Yefremov insists that the state should
clearly define what kinds of armaments will be devel-
oped, and should issue firm state orders, not permitting
this year’s chaos to be repeated.

At the moment the entire state system is working against
our national interests, the defense industry man

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

believes—there is no discipline, and half of our funds are
“going untaxed” [khodit bez nalogov]. But the tax ser-
vices are worried about poking their noses into commer-
cial structures’ affairs, preferring to flegce the plants.
Consequently, he does not believe that lending rates wiil
fall from 180 percent to 20 percent.

'n his concluding speech B.N. Yeltsin tried to reconcile
opponents and smooth over differences. He also prom-
ised a tough financial and credit policy and the preser-
vation of the technological base and high-tech produc-
tion facilities.

Conference Viewed

MM2911143594 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
