Russian 29 Nov 94 p 2

[Article by Lyudmila Biryukova: “Step from Confronta-
tion Toward Solidary Responsibility’’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The hardships of the reforms
have exhausted the Russians. But ahead lies one more
complex stage following an unforgiving route.

Of course the tough 1995 budget is no picnic. It has
already been called a Pinochet budget. That is human
nature: to look about in the face of each difficult obstacle
to see whether there is some way round or whether it is
possible to turn back.

It is not possible. We are past the point of no return.
Many speakers said this from the conference rostrum.
But advancing with tiny steps when our legs are weighed
down by the unstable quagmire of the slow-moving crisis
has also become unbearable. We need a resolute break-
through on the path toward stabilization. That is what
the government proposes to do in 1995.

It is hard to embark on a breakthrough when some
people are dragging us to the left, some to the right, and
others calling for chaos and insubordination, when the
situation requires self-discipline. Can society understand
the harsh need for a new stage of reforms?

The answer to this difficult questicn should have been
given by the all-Russian economic conference convened
at President B. Yeltsin’s initiative. Representatives of
the legislative and executive power, leaders of the
regions and of public organizations, businessmen, scien-
tists, figures from the opposition parties and groupings
were there. But only one of the speakers shed tears for
the planned system. G. Yefremov, director of a large
defense enterprise, turning to the prime minister sitting
in the presidium, reminded him that even in the A’ ab
Emirates, from which V. Chernomyrdin had just
returned, a planning organ—the Ministry of the Econ-
omy—holds first place. To which the premier replied:
“The sultan is in first place there” (laughter in the
auditorium). And President B. Yeltsin added that even
members of parliament are appointed by the sultan.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying axid dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Despite the fact that in our country the State Planning
Committee has been abolished and the parliament is
elected, represent: .g various strata of society, the con-
ference did reach agreement on the main thing: The
government’s tough new financial policy is not some
routine tightening of the screws but an essential stage of
the reform that has become irreversible. Contrary to the
opposition’s pessimistic forecasts, the economy has not
sunk into hyperinflation. At the same time half-measures
and a slow, gradual approach in the struggle against
inflation have exhausted thermselves. They merely
increase the economic and social expenditure of stabili-
zation policy.

Things are obvious. But however many examples we
have seen in recent years of the obvious being not merely
doubted but denied, sometimes ostentatiously and
fiercely so. This time it was different. “It was heart-
warming when both speakers of the Federal Assembly
chambers—V. Shumeyko and I. Rybkon—supported the
government’s idea in general and as a whole (although
not without reservations), L. Polezhayev, head of the
Omsk Oblast Administration, said from the rostrum. It
looks as though a limit has been set to the seemingly
endless confrontation between the president, govern-
ment, and parliament.

Indeed, in his speech State Duma Chairman I. Rybkin
stressed that the discussion of the draft budget in parlia-
ment was even-tempered and businesslike. It was
decided to work through the draft once again with the
participation of government representatives.

Anyone who expected an outbreak of parliamentary
anger was probably very disappointed. Nothing terrible
happened and it may be hoped will not happen. The
draft will be tested for faults and pressure and will be
supplemented and enriched with new proposals, which
will only benefit it. The main thing is that the govern-
ment does not intend to retreat from its general line and
the deputies are very well aware of that.

The speakers warned the country’s leadership against
using the fact that the budget has not been adopted as a
threat to dissolve parliament. The duma has only just
entered the rhythm of lawmaking work. It is doubtful
whether its disbandment would improve the situation in
thg country. New elections will hardly end in victory for
the progressive reformist forces: The situation is dif-
ferent now. Does the country really need Mytishchi on
an all-Russian scale? one speaker asked rhetorically,
having in mind the elections near Moscow won by the
notorious Mavrodi.

Actually, one so wants to hope that the Duma has
embarked on a course toward constructive work by no
means because of the potential threat of dissolution but
because it is maturing and gaining experience.

“The budget’s toughness has dumbstruck some people,”
the prime minister said in his report, and this “dumb-
struck state” was obviously manifested at the confer-
ence, especially in disputes in the corridors. Journalists

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 21

surrounded G. Yavlinskiy, who said that the government
has stated good aims but they cannot be achieved. He
has formed the impression that the draft budget was
written by the narrators of fairy stories. The big deficit
cannot be overcome by the measures outlined in the
draf:—that is the well-known economist’s opinion.

Representatives of the business world think completely
differently.

“We welcome the idea of covering ine budget deficit
through noninflation sources,” M. Masarskiy said on
behalf of businessmen. Moreover, he gave an assurance
that businessmen have specific proposals as to how to
make the budget’s revenue section feasible. For instance,
how to expand the scale of the sale of state securities and
how to make internal loans attractive and not only to
corporate bodies but also to individuals. So that people
are not diverted by “pyramid” games offered them uy
some swindlers.

The speeches by representatives of the state and nonstate
sectors of the economy nonetheless offered a striking
contrast. The former’s motif is well known: Give us such
and such. The latter were just the opposite: Take such
and such. Businessmen for instance suggested to the
government a program for how to return to the country
the capital which has fled abroad and for what must be
done for this. “And foreign capital will be bound to come
to Russia with it and extensive investments in our
economy will begin,” said M. Masarskiy.

Businessmen might seem to have no concern for the
country’s foreign debts. But it turns out they have
proposals as to how to ease that heavy burden by using
Russia’s real estate abroad and also the Russian gold
kept in banks in France and Japan. The government
should seize these proposals and put them to use but the
apparatus has been examining them slowly and phleg-
matically for nearly two years now. For instance,
recently the first practical step was taken: An interde-
partmental commission has been created to resolve the
problem. But while the government has been thinking
and hesitating Japan and France have made the figures
for Russian gold a secret.

The entrepreneurs also have ideas about how to deal
with our debtors, that is those countries which owe us
money. To wait for Saddam Husayn to pay up is highly
irrational. We must work with the debts of third coun-
tries, we must sell them, and no one knows better than
entrepreneurs how to do that. But so far their proposals
remain unused.

Yes, debates were not smooth at the conference. The
government was criticized from various sides. There
were demands that it improve management, reduce
expenditure on the apparatus, simplify the tax system,
equalize budget provision for different regions, expand
incentives to entrepreneurial activity.... Perhaps those
assembled were most alarmed by the government's

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

22 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

intentions to render investment support only to effec-
tively operating enterprises. Will that not lead to even
bigger structural distortions: to the raw material orien-
tation of the economy and the loss of part of our
scientific and technical potential? We must be concerned
for science-intensive sectors even under tough budget
conditions—that was the overall message. Representa-
tives of commercial banks voiced the readiness to take
the most active part in supporting high-technology pro-
duction facilities. Innovations Commercial Bank leader
V. Vinogradov announced that it is already giving
credits to the aircraft, instrument building, and chemical
industries. And bankers in the free sector of the economy
are not throwing their money to the wind.

The business-like tone of the talk at the conference made
us forget for a while our society’s politicized nature.
Especially as no one from the opposition asked to speak,
although S. Glazyev, Yu. Skokov, and other opponents
of the president and government were in the auditorium.
That means that the authorities only have to switch the
talk into the channel of real matters for the most impla-
cable to lose their tongues. They have nothing to suggest.
And one starts to understand what they are seeking: only
power for themselves. Because whoever is in the minis-
terial seats will be obliged to pursue the same tough
financial policy, to which there is no alternative today.

And one more conclusion from this big talk: Society’s
intellectual elite is by no means opposed to reforms. And
natural differences over partial issues can and must be
overcome. Including at forums like these.

Chernomyrdin Addresses Conference

44M2911121594 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 29 Nov 94 First Edition p 3

[““Abridged” version of report by Russian Prime Min-
ister V.S. Chernomyrdin at 26 November All-Russia
Economic Conference in the Kremlin: “Next Year, 1995,
Wiil Be The Most Cruciai’’]

[FBIS Translated Text] The third year of the economic
reform in Russian—1994—is almost over. One can
definitely say that a great deal of ground has been
covered. Everything that has happened and is happening
in the national economy affects the interests of every
citizen. And I am sure I am not mistaken when I say that
no one in the country is indifferent when it comes to the
economic situation.

What is special about this moment is that we are now in
a situation where we are entering a new stage of eco-
nomic transformations and new, even more difficult
tasks are emerging. From this viewpoint, 1995 will
probably be the most crucial and the most intensive,
because the consistency of the policy and the correctness
and decisiveness of the actions will determine how soon
Russians will see tangible and convincing evidence of
emergence from the economic crisis and the beginning of
the country’s stable, steady development.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Safeguarding development of this kind is, strictly
speaking, the main idea hehind the economic reform.
The entire concept of the new medium-term economic
policy program is geared to this.

There is yet another not insignificant fact we must not
lose sight of—it is the challenge of history, the challenge
of the future. Russia must respond to this challenge ini
the manner that most befits a great power.

In only 265 weeks mankind will be entering the 21st
century. And we must be among the frontrunners when
we get there, not among the civilized countries’ back-
markers.

This is ultimately a matter of national pride. Herein lies
the true meaning of Russian patriotism at the present, to
be frank, difficult stage of our country’s development.

This, if you like, is the chief moral lesson to be drawn
from what has already been done in the sphere of
economic reform.

Finally, it is time we stopped trying to frighten ourselves
with endless “national disasters.’ Of course, we are not
going to overcome the industrial decline, the inadequate
provision for millions of fellow citizens, the threat of
unemployment, nonpayments, and much else immedi-
ately. The situation is such that people have already
experienced the full weight of all the negative aspects of
the breakup of the old economy and have been only
lightly touched by the positive things the new economy is
bringing with it.

But there have been real advances. The economic life of
the country and its citizens presents a varied picture.
Russia is assimilating the principles and laws of the
market and is building a normal life.

From this viewpoint 1994 is not simply a model year, it is
a watershed.

What we can say today quite definitely is that the reform
is now founded on a qualitatively new economy, the
functioning of which is determined by the action of
market regulators.

The most striking indicator of this is the trend that has
emerged toward the stabilization of production volumes.
Since September we have had a pretty steady monthly
increase in industrial output. In November it is 4 percent
compared with October. The number of enterprises that
have experienced lengthy (that is, more than one shift)
production stoppages has fallen. Losses of working time
fell from 26 million man-days in September to 20 in
October. This is lower than in all previous months of the
year.

As a result we expect a GDP of 620-630 trillion rubles
[R] by the end of 1994. That is 85 percent of last year’s
volume in comparable prices.

The course of inflationary processes has changed mark-
edly. In 1992 prices on the consumer market increase 26

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

times, in 1993 they increased nine times, and in 1994 the
increase will be 2.8-2.9 times. And this takes into
account the prices explosion following so-called “Black
Tuesday,” which amounted to 15 percent in October as
a whole. But already in November we have seen the
figure fall to 12-13 percent. I think we will see a fall in
December too.

There have been some obvious changes in the social
sphere too. In 1993 real incomes increased by 10 per-
cent, and in the first 10 months of this year they
increased by 18.5 percent. All this is evidence of the
growing influence of effective demand on the macro-
economy. It is manifested, in particular, in the fact that
retail trade turnover in the current year (in comparable
prices) is right up to the 1990 level. The volume of sales
of nonfood commodities is surging ahead. The structure
of food consumption is also changing. In 1994, according
to surveys of domestic budgets, fur the first time in three
years consumption of meat and meat products and fruit
increased.

More than 20 percent of people’s money has gone into
savings this year, and their economic composition is now
fundamentally different from what it was three-four
years ago. Formerly the main reason for c:.. ngs was that
there was simply nothing to spend money on. The
situation is different now. Savings are made in order to
buy securities and real estate, to build a house, and so on.
Here is just one example—by the beginning of October
3.2 million square meters of housing had been con-
structed at people’s expense. This is one-third more than
in 1993.

The formation of qualitatively new market institutions has
been a central feature of 1994. By October 1994 around
108,000 state and municipal enterprises in Russia had
cnanged their form of ownership to collective or private.
As a result, for the first time in many decades, the
nonstate sector is the dominant one in the economy.

Privatization has entered a new, monetary stage. Around
50 enterprises have been through the proper procedure
and been declared bankrupt. The currency and stock
markets have started operating, although not without
hiccups.

Of course, this is just the beginning. Many of the things
we included in our plan of action for 1994, that were
incorporated in the president’s message to the Federal
Assembly, were not fully realized. For instance, the
decline in production and in technological standards in a
number of sectors was not halted. All is far from well in
the conversion sphere.

The transition to the new stage of privatization has not
yet had a decisive effect on the attraction of investments,
sales of shares remain low, and psychological barriers
and public wariness of bankruptcy have not been over-
come.

The formulation of a modern legislative base to regulate
the securities market and banking is well behind

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 23

schedule. The cost of this is evident—a series of scandals
involving various “collectors” of the people’s money
and, in the end, fraud.

In the latter half of the year, unfortunately, income
differentiation started to increase again and the reduc-
tion in the number of our fellow citizens below the
poverty line (around 30 million) slowed down.

We have felt and are continuing to feel the full force of
incomplete institutional transformations in the Russian
economy in the exacerbation of the problem of nonpay-
ments. As of 1 October 1994 overdue credit indebtedness
in the economy totaled R71.8 trillion and overdue debit
indebtedness totaled R65.6 trillion. These problems are
continuing to grow and there has been no reduction
either in the total number of enterprises with overdue
debts. They number around 50,000.

This state of affairs directly affects the implementation of
the federal budget for 1994. Its income base is shaky due
to a significant shortfall in tax revenue. So budget
expenditure has had to be slashed in all main areas.

We sought to take all these lessons into acccount in our
work on the draft budget for 1995.

For 1994 as a whole we expect revenue of around R70
trillion and expenditure of a little over R140 trillion.
And I can assure you that all overdue budget debts
relating to pay will be cleared by the government before
the end of this year. So compared with September we will
see a threefold reduction in the debt for pay and service-
men’s financial upkeep alone.

The government’s operational commission on
improving payments and settlements has been operating
for some months now. A number of legislative and
normative acts has been prepared on the introduction of
bills of exchange circulation and Finance Ministry trea-
sury bills and on the rules for the local mutual oir-etting
of enterprises’ debts at regional level.

Of course, these are just the first steps toward stabiliza-
tion. And we are very well aware that proper payment
and settlement arrangements in the country depend on
the simultaneous solution of other problems, such as the
restructuring of the economy; the improvement of taxa-
tion, price and customs policy, and the banking system;
the development of the securities market; and the estab-
lishment of rigorous financial discipline. The govern-
ment will be concentrating on these areas in 1995.

Of course, the results of the year, and of the whole three
years of the reforms, cannot be boiled down to the
dynamics of economic indicators. It is extremely impor-
tant that we have started to acquire a better under-
standing of the actual logic of transformations.

Hence stem several fundamental conclusions incorpo-
rated by the government in the blueprint for the Eco-
nomic Policy Program in 1995-1997.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

24 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Above all, it is clear that the reform has reached a
watershed. Of course, the crisis is not yet over, but the
stage of crisis development is nearly past. And whereas
previously we really were unable to overcome or even
halt the crisis, now we have the opportunity to do so. We
have moved on from the phase of general production
decline to the phase of structural crisis. The present and
future of industry are determined primarily by demand.
And this means that restructuring is not merely inevi-
table, but it has actually begun, albeit rather slowly. But
this is movement of a different kind—movement for-
ward.

New correlations between domestic prices have been
established. As they approach world prices, they are now
mainly based on supply and demand.

As a result we are faced with the full-blown problems of
production costs and the competitiveness of Russian
industry.

Consequently, for the first time in three years questions
not only of survival, but of the deliberate improvement
of labor productivity and production efficiency on a new
market basis have started to emerge. These almost for-
gotten words are returning to the vocabulary of industri-
alists and entrepreneurs.

There has been a sharp increase in the need for invest-
ments to replace and update the apparatus of produc-
tion. Again there is a real basis for this in the economy—
the process of accumulation, which has resumed. There
is money in the country: in the accounts of enterprises
and banks and in the hands of the population. But, to be
frank, the mechanisms whereby it passes into invest-
ments are extremely inadequate and unstable. The lack
of progress in the solution of this key problem is the
main brake on the reforms at the moment.

The second very important conclusion is that the new
stage of the reforms urgently requires new approaches in
all aspects of our policy. The lessons of 1994 can be
summed up briefly as follows.

First, the moderately tough financial policy pursued in the
past two years played a certain positive role but has
exhausted its potential. The balance between fighting
inflation and supporting enterprises so as to give them
time to adapt to market conditions cannot go on indef-
initely.

Second, financial stabilization is not only budgetary and
fiscal policy. It is not least but perhaps even more the
institutional section of the reform: privatization for cash,
the solution of the nonpayments problem, bankruptcy,
the formation of a stock market, a stable exchange rate,
and the development of the banking system.

In-depth transformations in the most budget-intensive
sectors of the economy and spheres of society are abso-
lutely essential in this connection. I shall be frank:
Without the real reform of the agrarian sector, the

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

defense complex, and the Armed Forces as a whole, we
will make no progress at all.

In principle the outlines of these transformations have
been drawn fairly clearly. With regard to the agrarian
sphere, here the state must provide comprehensive assis-
tance to developing the market infrastructure in the
countryside. We will resolve the problem of the under-
developed system of commercial seasonal credit pledged
against both the future harvest and the land. The armory
of the agrarian reform includes the development of the
system of forward contracts to ensure state needs and
incestives for private investments in the processing
industry.

As for our defense complex, we will primarily defend a
realistic approach to maintaining the Russian Armed
Forces based on their present-day state and the imme-
diate future.

It is clear that we are going to carry out no: simply a
mechanical reduction of military production units and
of the army as a whole but will reform this sphere.

Third, a market reform in Russia can succeed only with
the consistent strengthening of the institutions of state
power.

Figuratively speaking, we have all thrown off the old
“greatcoat” of imperial statehood. Even today there are
many who are greatly tempted to don that coat again and
furthermore fasten all its buttons and restore the whole
arsenal of state regulation.

It is obvious today that the existing system of organs of
state management in the economic sphere is beginning to
slow down the progress of the transformations. That
means that this system’s useful life is coming to an end
and that we have to conduct a major reform of it. We have
the direct mandate of President B.N. Yeltsin to do that
and have already begun work in that direction.

Since we have moved away from the old command
system there is only one path to economic growth—it can
only be achieved via trust in market forces and thus via
a stable ruble, low market interest rates, property guar-
antees, a falling inflation rate, and new mechanisms of
state influence on the economy working only in those
areas where market self-regulation either js not working
or still cannot work to full effect.

It is on that basis that we determine the main priorities
of 1995-1997. Namely: financial stabilization, private
investments, and proper law and order.

This, I am sure, fully meets the strategic goals of eco-
nomic policy outlined in the president’s message “On
Strengthening the Russian State.” Specifically, it serves
the continuation of the reform, the change of the very
structure of the economic system, and the easing of the
social cost of transformations.

We were guided by these tasks when we determined the
rationale of the development of the reform over the next

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

three years. Analysis shows that we have every opportu-
nity to make 1995 the year of the financial stabilization of
the Russian economy. To make 1996 the year that we
emerge from the depression and create the preconditions
for economic upturn. And make 1997 the year of the active
policy of stable economic growth.

Of course, the implementation of this plan will mean a
very tight schedule for the government literally every
working day. But we are convinced that our plans are
feasible. Unless we achieve the breakthrough now,
tomorrow we will be dragged ever deeper into the mire of
a “sluggish crisis” out of which it will be even harder and
more painful to drag ourselves.

the longer we wallow in this mire the longer the oppres-
sive state of “hidden inflation” will persist, the longer we
will labor under debilitating 15-20 percent inflation a
month, and the less hope the ordinary citizen will have of
a normal and predictable “tomorrow.”

The experience of recent years shows that we can only
expect to succeed if we do not lose sight of the social
thrust of the transformations.

Ultimately this is the most true and reliable guideline.
That is why the government believes that the main aim
of its social policy over the next three years is to stabilize
people’s living standard and ensure that it rises while
easing the burden of the crisis on the most vulnerable
strata.

In addition to targeting social assistance better to those
who really need it, main attention will be devoted to
encouraging the labor and entrepreneurial activity of the
economically active section of society. To this end the
government is going to:

—create the conditions to boost pay by a phased lifting
of the restrictions on the use of enterprises’ consump-
tion funds;

—pursue, within the framework of the fiscal reform, a
moderately tough policy of taxation on high and very
high personal incomes and encouraging their use for
investment purposes;

—ensure an effective system for the socioeconomic
protection of hired workers which include: mecha-
nisms for the prompt payment of wages atic guaran-
teed employment for redundant workers on the basis
of a state system of retraining and skills improvement.

The priority of social policy is the stabilization of funding
for the social sphere. To this end there will be a demar-
cation of responsibility between the federal and local
organs of power for the implementation of social policy
based on the corresponding agreements between the
government and the components of the Russian Feder-
ation.

Ofcours .* government does not intend to stop at that.
In 1995-1597 we are going to focus our efforts on the
three most important social problems whose solution

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 25

will very largely determine what I would call the whole
future appearance of Russian society.

First, there is the implementation of the program to help
restore the population’s savings, first and foremost for the
older age groups.

Second, there is the implementation of the housing reform
and the formation of a real housing market. That is
closely bound up with incentives for housing construction
making the maximum use of the population’s resources
and major state support.

Third, the government intends to work with the regions
in 1995-1997 to achieve major progress in resolving the
difficult problem of the transfer of enterprises’ social
sphere to local organs of power.

Efforts aimed at implementing the federal anticrime
program and the president’s corresponding edicts are
being sharply stepped up today. A special state fund is
being set up to provide financial support for all the
necessary measures here. Our undoubted priority is to
increase the social status and socio-legal protection of
staffers of the law enforcement agencies.

However, the executive alone cannot do it all here. Our
legislation lags behind present-day requirements, 2nd the
adoption of vitally-needed laws and codes is clearly
overdue. A fundamental legal reform must be imple-
mented without delay. The same goes for the creation in
the country of a judicial system which corresponds to the
spirit and letter of the Russian Constitution.

Democracy must be able to defend itself! Our direct duty
to Russians is to put an end to the anarchy created by
crime.

The character of economic development today is deter-
mined less by the aims than by the mechanisms with
which they are achieved. And that means by the answers
to the questions how, in what way, by what means, and
when.

By the way, it would be a good idea if that were borne in
mind by those “friends of the people” in the State Duma
who recently adopted a decision on sharply increasing
the minimum wage by more than 150 percent. Tell me:
Where are we to find the extra R6.5 trillion a month
needed to increase wages in the budget-funded sphere
alone?

You see, the whole system of labor remuneration in the
national economy will actually be “blown to pieces.” The
extra money pumped into the economy each month will
inevitably lead to a rise in prices and restore inflation to
the now pretty forgotten level of the middle of 1992.

That is what such “concern for the people” means!

From this standpoint, there are three features of funda-
mental importance in 1995: financial policy in close
coordination with the actions of the Central Bank, a

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

26 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

clear approach to regulation in the sphere of prices and
pricing, and encouragement of investment.

All participants in the conference are in possession of the
basic documents concerning tax and budgetary policy.
There is no point in my repeating myself, therefore. Our
position is well known.

Yes, the tough budget could be even tougher. Yes, we
still have work to do on it. We are not dogmatists. The
government is prepared to make compromises with the
State Duma and the Federation Council. Both the struc-
tures of the revenue and expenditure side [of the budget]
and the total sums involved could change somewhat. We
will be working on this within the framework of the the
conciliation commission.

There are, however, fundamental things where compro-
mises will inevitably lead merely to a worsening of the
overall situation. I would put the size of the budget deficit
and the means of financing it first and foremost in thut

category.

We can no longer rely on inflationary sources of state
expenditure financed by Central Bank credit.

I would like to stress once again that covering the budget
deficit through the state securities market and the
resources of international financial organizations is an
act of deliberate choice by the government. Of course,
this is a megatask. But times are such now that if we do
not set ourselves these kind of megatasks we will make
no real progress at all.

Let us remember how back in 1993 many skeptics
forecast that short-term state bonds would be a
resounding failure and dismissed the very idea as pure
fantasy. /

But the facts are that we began with a modest R230
billion in 1993, while today more than R12.5 trillion of
short-term state bonds have been placed.

The same applies to the question of the reality of the size
of the revenue and expenditure included in the budget.
The second half of the year has already shown that, while
tax collection is going slowly, the situation is neverthe-
less improving. Of course, nonpayments and the sheer
number of tax breaks, which is unprecedented in world
practice, are having a most substantial impact on the
extent of the arrears. But who said that in 1995, when the
tax reform starts, that this will remain as it is?

It is incontrovertible that today state expenditure is the
main source for transfusing money into the economy.
And the main problem is not the size of the expenditure
but ensuring that it gets to the intended recipient, that
strict control is exercised over its targeted use, and that it
is evenly financed.

This is where we see our main potential for reorganizing
and rationalizing expenditure policy. Therefore, for all
the toughness of the budget, which, understandably,

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

staggers many of our opponents, it can be fulfilled, since
the whole economic policy of 1995 is essentially working
to that end.

The government is far from wearing blinders here. We
are open to serious professional discussion. But here too
we have principles. We will not be able to accept
proposals which, while purporting to clarify the priori-
ties of structural policy, amount as a rule to continuing
to pump money into the economy in order to prolong the
existence of unviable production structures, even if they
are ones which are dear to our hearts. Nor will we be able
to accept the standard incantations about the need to
freeze prices and increase state orders, which also ulti-
mately leads to the same pmuping up of the economy
with worthless, inflation-creating money. That would be
a an irresponsible decision.

Therefore there can be no question of freezing prices or
mandatory regulation of prices: The more such regula-
tion there is, the greater the threat of price acceleration.
Free prices must become established once and for all in
the markets in consumer goods and agricultural produce.
At the same time decisions have been made to toughen
up price policy somewhat in the electricity generating
industry, the gas industry, and railroad and pipeline
transport, that is to say, wherever natural monopolies
exist.

Investment policy is a specially important question. Bud-
getary resources here are extremely limited and, more-
over, are geared for the most part toward financing plans
and programs of social import. This line is being main-
tained throughout the 1995-1997 period.

Hence a twofold conclusion follows. First, centralized
state investment aimed at production must yield the
maximum return and attract private business invest-
ment. And appropriate mechanisms for competitive
placment of investment and joint state-commercial
financing of highly efficient projects are envisaged for
this purpose.

Second, the center of gravity in investment policy is
shifting toward so-called indirect and institutional mea-
sures of regulation. There are a great number of oppor-
tunities here.

We must frankly acknowledge that we have used them
extremely unsatisfactorily to date: And these are the
practice of accelerated amortization, various fiscal
methods, investment tenders as part of postcheck priva-
tization, the secondary issuing of shares, and specific
work designed to attract the population’s resources into
bank deposit accounts, pension funds, insurance compa-
nies, and so forth.

We will be actively seeking to attract foreign as well as
domestic investment.

It can be said without exaggeration that we are talking
about a major new area of state policy, one generated by
objective changes in the economic situation. And this

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

concentration on encouraging investment is indeed a most
important feature of the new government program.

In conclusion I would like to say that the fate of any
policy ultimately depends on what it gives people. Our
answer to this question is as follows: stable prices, steady
and reliable incomes, secure pensions and savings,
growth in real wages and consumption, new jobs through
increasing investment, more opportunities for enterprise
and initiative, increasing law and order, and the personal
safety of citizens.

This is the social thrust of what we are proposing. Here is
the main criterion for assessing the decisions made.
Here, if you wish, lies the moral core of professionalism
in any sphere of activity bound up in some way with the
economy.

Economic reform and everything connected with it
always means pretty dry words, computations, figures,
and so forth. But behind them lies something else.
Behind them lies the life of Russia and its future. And
that is our common concern. There can be no detached
observers here. Mutual trust and responsibility, cooper-
ation, and joint work on the mechanisms for overcoming
the crisis constitute the most important political compo-
nents and conditions of success.

We are capable of seizing this opportunity, this chance.

Efficacy of Conference Questioned
MM2911161194 Moscow TRUD in Russian 29 Nov 94
pp 1-2

[Article by Vitaliy Golovachev: “All Quiet in Kremlin
Auditorium. Will Russian Republican and Regional
Leaders’ Trip to Moscow Be Worth It?”’}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Where are we up to in the course
of reform, and what direction should we be heading in?
Have there been any mistakes and, if so, how can they be
corrected? Those are the main questions which Russians
are trying to take on board today and about which
feelings are running high. The State Duma, which has
caused the government some problems recently, failed to
pass the draft budget for 1995 proposed by the Cabinet
on first reading. Opinion polls show a wide diversity of
opinions in society on the question of reform. Alarming
negative trends are continuing to grow alongside positive
shoots. In this complex and contradictory situation a
great deal was expected of the most representative and
large-scale economic conference of recent times, which
was held in the Kremlin last Saturday under the chair-
manship of President B. Yeltsin. Members of the gov-
ernment, leaders of the republics and regions of the
Russian Federation, Federal Assembly committees and
commissions, and federal executive organs, and direc-
tors of industrial enterprises, the construction complex,
banks, funds, consumer societies, corporations, compa-
nies, and associations took part in it. Also in attendance
were plenipotentiary representatives of the Russian Fed-
eration president, the leaders of top judicial organs, the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 27

General Prosecutor's Office, and the Central Electoral
Commission, workers from the Presidential Staff and the
government apparatus, and trade union leaders. In all
there were 650 people. The balcony at the Kremlin’s
Marble Hall was chockablock with Russian and foreign
journalists who were expecting, if not sensations, at least
an in-depth discussion of ways to solve our acute prob-
lems and the prospects for economic development.

But, in my view, these expectations were not fully borne
out. There was no acute debate or in-depth analysis of
the situation. During a number of the speeches I caught
myself thinking that what was happening was too remi-
niscent of the famous party and economic aktiv sessions
of the past. Most of the speakers (with few exceptions)
voiced restrained optimism and supported the general
line, by tradition raising a few local or general issues
(along the lines of “we need to improve this,” “‘it is
necessary to accelerate that,” and so forth). For some
reason no “dissidents”—that is, people with different
views about the progress of reform—spoke in the
Kremlin. So it can boldly be said that “the forum was
held in an atmosphere of unanimity and mutual under-
standing.” Which, however, by no means reflects the
breakdown of forces or the attitudes to what is hap-
pening prevalent among political circles and in society.

Against the general, rather ““amorphous” backdrop of the
conference, the short and very clearly prioritized intro-
ductory and closing speeches by the president stood out
clearly. Noting that the “situation is changing” and that
“there have been definite improvements,” Boris Yeltsin
stressed that “1994 was not lost for reform” and “the
Russian economy is starting to operate on the basis of
the laws of the market.” While continuing the reforms,
he said, it is very important to simultaneously lower the
social cost of economic transformations. This is
becoming the yardstick of the efficiency of the actions of
all organs of power. The most important tasks for the
coming period were stated. In particular, these are to
slow inflation as much as possible, to stabilize produc-
tion, to protect the interests of Russian producers on the
domestic and foreign markets, to create favorable con-
ditions for raising domestic and foreign investment
(primarily, private investment), to prevent the collapse
of leading-edge technologies and high-tech production
facilities, to restore “elementary order” in the country....

The premier’s report (announcement?) created a two-
fold impression. On the one hand, Viktor Stepanovich
said some very correct things; on the other hand, there
were a lot of general views and, I think, not enough
analysis of specific ways to solve our current highly acute
problems. Reaffirming yet again the adherence to the
market and the government's intention to move from a
moderately tough to a tougher policy, the premier dwelt
on positive and negative trends. The positive trend is the
increase in Output volume in recent months, the reduced
losses of worktime in October, the growth in real
incomes, retail trade turnover, and meat consumption,
the level of housing construction, including construction

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

28 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

financed out of the population’s own funds.... The neg-
ative points are the defaulting crisis, the technological
“retreat,” the lack of investment, and the inadequacy of
institutional transformations.

To summarize—“reform has reached a critical point,”
“the phase of crisis development is near completion.”
But it is well known that opponents (and not only
antimarket “‘conservatives”) have repeatedly voiced
their objections and adduced counterarguments on these
points. The conference continually heard some well-
known optimistic claims—for instance, that there is
every opportunity that 1995 will be a year of financial
stabilization, that 1996 will see the emergence from
depression and the appearance of the prerequisites for a1
upturn, and that 1997 will mark the start of steady
economic growth.

So who is against this? The question lies elsewhere: How
are these goals to be achieved? Our government has very
often been wrong in its forecasts and in its assessments of
the situation. And the economic forecast for the current
year, 1994, turned out to be wrong—the situation in the
country has developed differently than “expected” in
terms of the inflation rate, the production slump, and a
number of other highly important indicators. We do not
have to go far for examples—two weeks ago the leaders
of the Ministry of Economics told me with complete
confidence that inflation in November-December would
be no more than 7-8 percent. But prices in November
have continued to rise rapidly—as they had in October.
Inflation this month has been double (!) the recent
forecast—standing at 13-15 percent (it was 11 percent in
the first three weeks of November). It turns out that the
Ministry of Economics cannot even provide an accurate
forecast for a few weeks ahead. Some natural questions
arise—just how reliable are long-term government plans
and how feasible are their goals?

Enterprise and organization accounts payable increased
once again to stand at 147.4 trillion rubles [R] at |
October, including R71.8 trillion in deferred debt (an
increase of 22.8 percent). Around 50,000 enterprises owe
money to the budget and to their suppliers. The crisis is
not being remedied but is continuing to deteriorate. Just
how will this most acute problem be resolved if the
measures being taken are no help? Are there any new
approaches and ideas? The issue remains “up in the air.”

Viktor Chernomyrdin sharply criticized the State Duma
decision to increase the minimum wage as of | January
next year. The arguments quoted were indeed weighty: It
will cost R15-16 trillion a month (R6.5 trillion for
““budget-funded” workers alone)}—where will that money
come from? “The entire system of labor remuneration
will be blown apart and inflation will increase—that is
what this ‘concern’ for the people means,” the premier
said bluntly. But there is something else we do not
understand. Why has there been silence about the fact
that 5 million Russians earned less than R50,000 in
September (there are no more recent figures available)}—
or half as much as the minimum wage?

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

The number of poor people in Russia (that is, the
population with incomes below the minimum wage) did
not fall but increased in October from 30.4 to 32 million.
These, incidentally, are the official figures of the Russian
State Committee for Statistics. Differentials continued
to deteriorate and the correlation in incomes between
the 10 percent of the most and least well-off sections of
the population increased from 10.8 (1993) to 13.5. And,
finally, one of the most “acute” social issues—the cur-
rent size of the minimum wage (R20,500)}—merely dis-
credits the powers that be, in my view, since anyone
earning that kind of money could only afford to live for
five days.

Not only is unemployment benefit equivalent to the
minimum wage being paid to tens of thousands of
unemployed people, but 200,000 workers earned less
than R25,000 in September. How can these “pariahs”™ in
their own country survive? Why has the state left them to
the whim of fate? Where is the promised presidential
program for combating poverty? In short, when the
premier sharply criticized the Duma for its desire to help
the “new poor,” it would have been logical if he had also
said that the government intends to resolve these highly
acute problems. Alas!.... Clearly, declaring that the social
thrust of reform will be strengthened is one thing, and
specific steps in that direction are another.

An acute and vitally important question for Russia is the
crime wave that is without precedent in the last 70 years.
Touching on this subject, Viktor Stepanovich exclaimed:
“Enough of keeping people in fear. We need to act, and
act resolutely!” I heard these true words and thought:
“Just who is stopping the president, the government, or
all of society from doing this?”

In conclusion I will quote a few remarks by conference
participants about government structures and the policy
that is being pursued. “Supporting only efficient produc-
tion facilities could lead to the integrity of the national
economy being violated and the loss of scientific and
technical potential” (V. Shumeyko). ““The aims stated in
the concept for 1995-1997 were put forward in 1992,
1993, and then in 1994 as well.... We will have to pay in
the future for our mounting foreign and domestic debt”
(I. Rybkin). “We must not be wrong by orders of
magnitude when we are giving state orders” (G. Yefre-
mov, general director of a science and production asso-
ciation from Reutov near Moscow). “The unreliability of
the authorities does not inspire confidence in them” (L.
Polezhayev, head of the Omsk Oblast Administration).

The “most representative” conference held in the
Kremlin should perhaps have demonstrated broad sup-
port in the country for the reform policy that is being
followed. In this it was partially successfully, but the
issues and the difficulties giving rise to confrontation
and tension in society remain. The conference was
addressed by eight other people apart from those already
mentioned. It all ended at 1400. Will this short event be
able to in any way convince political opponents and the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

tens of millions of unhappy people in Russia. Or might it
partly have the opposite effect? Was it rational to have
republican and regional leaders converge and to convene
such busy people just to hear a few speeches?

Duma Could Obstruct Chernomyrdin on Economy

MM2911142794 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
29 Nov 94 p 4

[Article by Otto Latsis: “Viktor Chernomyrdin Casts Off
the Old Greatcoat. How Will Parliament Respond to the
Premier’s Challenge?’’}

{[FBIS Translated Text] “Market reforms can only suc-
ceed if there is a consistent strengthening of the institu-
tions of state power,” said Viktor Chernomyrdin in his
report at the conference “Prospects of the Economic
Reforms in Russia” in the Kremlin 26 November.

These words hardly pleased the proponents of the tradi-
tional “state” approach [“‘derzhavnost’’] in the opposi-
tion camp because they were followed by an explanation
of this thesis which ran as follows: ““We have all thrown
off the old ‘mantle’ of strict statehood. Even today there
are many who are greatly tempted to don that coat again
and furthermore fasten all its buttons and restore the
whole arsenal of state regulation.... Since we have moved
away from the old command system there is only one
path to economic growth—it can only be achieved via
trust in market forces and thus via a stable ruble, low
market interest rates, property guarantees, a falling infla-
tion rate, and new mechanisms of state influence on the
economy.”

The conference was held in the Marble Hall—the former
CPSU Central Committee sessions hall—and in the
corridors people were joking that it was like a party
economic aktiv meeting. However, on this occasion the
formal similarity did not reflect the essence of the
matter. Akti meetings in days of yore were held to hand
out “rebukes” and to transmit instructions “from the top
down.” At this conference it was the executive in the
form of the president (who chaired the session) and the
premier which appealed to those below it for support.
And on the whole it got support, albeit not unconditional
support. Apart from those who, ex-officio, had a natural
right to speak (the chairmen of the chambers of parlia-
ment and the heads of the Finance Ministry and the
Central Bank) the floor was also given to the leaders of
the republics and oblasts and entrepreneurs. Judging by
the content of the speeches, if those chosen were sup-
porters of the position of the president and the premier
the selection was not very rigorously made. In addition
to the clear pro-market speeches of Anatoliy Lisitsin,
head of the Yaroslavl Oblast Administration, and the
entrepreneur Mark Masarskiy there were also speeches
which barely concealed a nostalgia for the “planned
economy.” It is especially remarkable that not one of the
speakers ventured to enter into a direct polemic with V.
Chernomyrdin’s call for a tough policy of financial
stabilization. Nor was it opposed by the chairmen of the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 29

chambers, even though it was obvious that the economic
views of both V. Shumeyko and I. Rybkin clearly differ
from those of V. Chernomyrdin.

I should add: Today’sChernomyrdin. Back in January
this year Viktor Stepanovich responded to criticism of
the government's financial policy from former cabinet
members Ye. Gaydar and B. Fedorov by refusing to take
a hard line. No, you cannot take every word he says
literally, and I do not intend to do so. The dynamic and
positive tone of the report did not prevent the use of the
following cautious phrase: The “moderately tough”
financial policy which has been pursued thus far was not
a mistake but has exhausted its potential. Thus far,
therefore, it has been correct, but in the future it will
cease to be. Things happen that way. Nevertheless, some
urgent tasks could be cited which have not been imple-
mented this year because of the lack of funds—perhaps
indeed because the money was spent on matters which
were not top priority.

Anatoliy Lisitsin talked about one of those tasks: The
regional authorities have not received support in
resolving a problem which the federal authorities
dumped entirely on the shoulders of the “localities” —
the transfer of social facilities from enterprises to munic-
ipalities. There is another no less important problem
which was mentioned vaguely and, apparently, in an
exclusively constructive sense by V. Chernomyrdin him-
self when he promised to begin “fulfilling the decisions”
on housing certificates and housing credits next year.
Thanks for that, but the decisions were adopted last
summer—was it necessary to wait six months?

However, the past is behind us now. Even the most
radical supporter of the reforms could only support the
premier’s current firm stance on the budget for the year
ahead. The specialists are arguing about the reliability of
the supposed sources of revenue and about how thor-
oughly the draft has been researched, but there is no
doubting the correctness of the fundamental tenet of the
remorseless fight against inflation and the rejection of
the printing press as a means of funding the budget
deficit. The only question is: Will that be appreciated by
the deputies, many of whom in developing their eco-
nomic consciousness clearly do not have that consider-
able experience which the head of the government dem-
onstrated before the Russian people’s eyes?

Some episodes from the Duma’s activities in recent days
make you fear that many deputies have still not been
cured of their infantile political disorders. One such
disorder is the decision (adopted on first reading) to
immediately double the minimum wage despite the
obvious lack of the financial resources to pay for it. In his
report at Saturday’s session, V. Chernomyrdin assessed
the “impact” of that decision at 15-16 trillion rubles a
month. In his opinion, any attempt to implement this
decision will wreck the whole wages system and cause a
new wave of inflation and currency speculation. The
only “consolation” is that, given the entrenched practice

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

30 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

of nonpayments—even the nonpayment of the state’s
obligations—this decision (if finally adopted) will simply
not be fulfilled with regard to the majority of hired
workers. The deputies themselves will be among the few
categories of citizens for whom this doubling of pay will
immediately become a reality. But the voters will have
time before the elections to work out what kind of games
people are playing around here.

I very much hope that parliament will not treat work
with the draft budget in the same casual manner. What-
ever else, the Kremlin conference provided quite a few
new arguments in support of the view that genuine
financial stabilization cannot be postponed any longer.

Targeted Funding for Farms Urged

MM2911111794 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 24
Nov 94 p 4

[Third and final part of IZVESTIYA analysis center
article under the rubric “Trillions for the Countryside:
Where Did They Go? Who Did They Go To? What Did
They Go On?”: “3. Bottomless Pit”; for parts one and
two, see pages 28-31 of 28 November Central Eurasia
DAILY REPORT under headline “Views Differ Greatly
Toward Agriculture Funding,” and pages 25-28 of 29
November Central Eurasia DAILY REPORT, “Ministry
Presents Controversial Farm Policy”]

[FBIS Translated Text] And so this year less than half the
planned sum was allocated for the needs of Russia’s
agro-industrial complex. But even that sum has proven
very impressive compared with other items of expendi-
ture and very tangible for the budget pocket. We have
already become convinced that over 8 trillion rubles [R]
have produced no noticeable effect primarily where they
were mostly directed. Moreover, a clear pattern has
emerged: The more substantial the investments in a
particular subsector the more obvious the regression and
production slump there. Under conditions when
everyone is short of the money required it would seem
that control over its use should be particularly strict. But
the situation in the regions attests to the opposite.

En Route from Moscow Money Shrinks, Gets Spilled,
and Simply Vanishes

Strange incidents have occurred over the entire year with
the transfer of funds promised to the regions and through
virtually all possible channels and items of financing.
Not a single agricultural campaign this year, according to
tie Russian Ministry of Agriculture, was financed on
time.

When the spring field work was already nearing comple-
tion funds for the sowing began to reach the farms. It is
primarily for this reason that the peasants did not
manage to complete the sowing within the optimum
agrotechnical deadlines, to apply a sufficient quantity of
fertilizers to the soil, or to ensure the effective operation
of equipment on the spring fields.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Only toward the end of the harvest did the main grain
regions start to receive money for bringing in the harvest.
Grain losses for this reason were up to 20 percent
according to the estimates of specialists from various
departments and analytical services.

The fall sowing was essentially a failure. Now we must
make up for the undersowing next spring, which we will
scarcely manage given the present financial relationship
between the countryside and the state.

The same situation exists with regard to food purchases
from peasants. According to our newspaper's analysis
center in the majority of the country’s regions money for
this purpose was received with a big delay and in sums
sometimes several times smaller than envisioned.

It would seem that there is a problem and that the
explanation for many of our agriculture’s troubles is the
state’s nonfulfillment of its financial obligations to the
peasants. But upon an attentive analysis of the system of
financing of agriculture and the effectiveness of the
expenditure of the funds received, the ways these funds
are passed on from the state to the peasants, and the way
in which these funds disappear en route, a completely
different picture takes shape.

Calculations prove that even the prompt receipt of funds
would only reduce slightly the overall losses and decline.
This money would still not be enough for truly effective
production and the solution of the problem of mass
nonpayments between partners in the agro-industrial
complex and the payment of wages to the complex’
workers. That is why a situation arises whereby the
money received locally starts to be spent by the local
authorities for purposes other than those envisaged. The
need to “plug the priority holes” as it were justifies the
local chiefs in their violations. But at the same time it
creates a situation of lack of control all along the chain as
the money passes from Moscow to the countryside. No
one seriously knows how much money has been spent,
when, and on what, where the money has gotten bogged
down, and how it was used.

Here is a characteristic situation. In Nizhniy Novgorod
Oblast subsidies were received very belatedly for pay-
ment of 30 percent of mineral fertilizers, for the pur-
chase and maintenance of pedigree livestock, and for raw
flax and hemp. The farms organized credits on the basis
of these subsidies, deposited the money and... paid large
fines for defaulting on payments. After all, the subsidies
began to reach the oblast finances department only in
late October.

But officials here were in no particular hurry to pay the
subsidies to the rayons. It is extremely complicated to
find out where the money interded for the peasants has
settled and is settled. It can only be supposed that
officials involved in the passage of funds are “playing”
with them in their own interests. But here they are
merely accepting the general rules of the game in agrot-
rillions. After all, the money’s route from Moscow to the
provinces is just as enigmatic and fraught with losses.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

For instance, subsidies for raw flax and hemp have not
reached Nizhniy Novgorod since the start of June, our
correspondent ascertained. What has happened? After all,
there are official notifications from the Russian Ministry
of Finances that R70 million was earmarked for the
oblast for these purposes 15 June and a further R99
million was earmarked 7 October. But this money has
not reached the oblast and it looks as though it will never
reach it now.

An independent investigation of the fate of the missing
money was undertaken by R. Ivanova, chief of the oblast
department of agriculture and land reform’s finances
and credits section.

“I traveled to Moscow three times to pick up the traces
of this money allegedly released from the federal budget
for the oblast,” she says. “I found out that the oblast
administration had, through the Russian Ministry of
Finances, organized a loan totalling R30 billion since the
previously envisaged funds for all our programs are not
being financed because of some overexpenditure of the
funds received by the oblast. The new loan went to cover
that overexpenditure. That, they said, was why we had
not been paid a kopek to compensate for expenditure on
electricity when raising vegetables in greenhouse condi-
tion, although at the Ministry of Finances I saw with my
own eyes a notification on the earmarking of R1.1 billion
to use for these purposes 27 July and a further R399
million 29 July. The situation is the same with other
items of expenditure. It is hard for me to judge the
purposes to which the huge loans taken by the oblast
administration, allegedly for the oblast agro-industrial
complex, were put. The only thing that is clear is that
they did not go to the countryside.”

Planned Confusion

If even local agrarian financial experts get lost trying to
guess the fate of the agrotrillions what can we say of the
peasants themselves? Through the efforts of our own
correspondents we carried out a special study on this
score. The conclusions everywhere were the same.

A poll by our correspondent in Penza Oblast among the
farm leaders and private farmers showed they were
completely uninformed as to the size of appropriations,
loans, and credits, and the principles for their allocation.
Almost none of those questioned could say what sums
should have been earmarked for the oblast and for farms
specifically and through which channels and for which
items of expenditure.

Actually, there is information widely known in the oblast
relating to machinations in local financing. In particular
at the Penzenskiy Rayon rural bank, founded by agricul-
tural enterprises and commercial structures, peasants
received credits at an annual interest rate of 230 percent
last year while about 50 leaders of various ranks were
made happy with credits at 8 percent.

In Yaroslavl Oblast, according to our correspondent, the
peasants have absolutely no knowledge of how much of

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 31

the agrotrillions has been earmarked for their oblast and
how much money should arrive, and for what.

The examples and those involved could be continued. It
is perfectly obvious that the present situation, in which
no one in the countryside can what are fundamentally its
own problems [as received] with the aid of the state in its
present framework and with its present principles, dis-
poses people toward many abuses and a new spiral of
corruption and bribery locally. What can we say of the
collective farms where the collective is traditionally
silent if there are many such cases even among private
farmers.

Here is a characteristic situation reported by our corre-
spondent from Ryazan Oblast. Here in Shilovskiy Rayon
a “private farmer,” V. Stepanenko, in actual fact an
invalid of the second group and a relation of the
chairman of the local “Respekt-bank,” received 100
hectares of land—she did not plow, did not sow, did not
harvest, but took advantage of preferential credit of R37
million, drives round in a “Volga” with her personal
chauffeur, and has acquired an apartment in Ryazan.
The bank itself received support here from Moscow
partners who emerged in this promising situation.

All this is very convenient for making money which is
still earmarked from the budget, and in astronomical
quantities moreover, but for the countryside as a whole it
is still not enough when an attempt is being made to
please everyone. Everyone is living with the sense that
they will not have enough money under any system and so
the money which is allocated is only feebly controlled.
Here it is the bureaucrat—in Moscow and locally—who
benefits as he sits in the path of the budget funds. The
dealers surrounding the bureaucrat benefit. The peasant
loses out. And that means primarily the peasant who
could feed us, the country.

Private Farmer Suffers from Racketeers and Even More
from the State

With present financing the most promising sectors and
forms of economic management lose out. Because when
everyone is given a little support, those which have no
future receive funds to prolong their agony while those
with a future are pushed toward their death.

This year private farmers increased their share in the
production of agricultural output with a drastic reduc-
tion in state financing. The increase in the efficiency and
revenue of private farms has attracted the attention of
criminal groupings. According to our information this
year there was a threefold increase in the number of
attacks on private farms. But the damage from this
cannot be compared with state financial misrule.
According to Association of Russian Peasant (Private)
Farms and Agricultural Cooperatives [AKKOR] figures,
this year an average of R159,000 in financial support
was paid to each private farm. That is many times less
than former state aid. But these are average figures. But
if you look and what the regions received, and how, the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

32 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

picture will prove completely different. Specialists from
AKKOR and our analytical center ascertained that in a
number of oblasts, in particular Kirov, Kostroma,
Ryazan, Sverdlovsk, and others, not a kopek had been
received from the state for the development of private
farming. Yet private farming is nonetheless developing
and increasing its share in agricultural production
output, including in these “cheated” regions.

But if something was received then it was spent in highly
dubious ways utterly in keeping with the general rules for
financing the countryside whereby more money is given
to those who work the worst and is directed toward areas
from which the return is the least possible.

In Vologda Oblast, for instance, the local authorities
assure everyone that private farming is developing well.

“Perhaps that is true,” our correspondent was told by
oblast AKKOR Chairman Sergey Yakovlev. “But only
with regard to farmer Olga Naumenko from Kirillovskiy
Rayon. Last year she was given credit of R100 million at
an annual interest rate of 8 percent and a further R50
million in free aid. And now eminent guests are taken to
visit her model farm. But last year I for one was able to
get only R25 million in credit at an interest rate of 103
percent, although the government promised 28 percent,
but at the bank I was told: If the bank gives us back the
difference in the interest rates we will pass it on to you.
For nine months I paid the bank 103 percent until I was
given back the difference in money that by that time was
devalued. But even that is not the point. What happens
is that if you are a private farmer you can count on
preferential credit, yet private farmers include many
drunkards and idlers. But why then waste the money, is
there so much money?”

No, there is not that much money. The Vologda farmer
is right. In its present form state support for private
farming and for the countryside as a whole is only being
discredited since it is not geared toward any economic
criteria or toward effectiveness.

In Rostov Oblast, for instance, they are also continuing
to fight to obtain subsidies for agricultural production
and the support of private farming. But even with
success in this struggle according to the calculations of our
correspondent and local AKKOR specialists no substan-
tial advances either at agricultural enterprises or in
private farming are predicted. Suffice it to say that wages
arrears for workers in the oblast’s agro-industrial com-
plex are now R42 billion and extinguishing those arrears
is utterly unfeasible.

The Countryside Needs Protectionism But Not As It
Exists Today

The facts attest that the confusion with financing the
Russian countryside reached frightening proportions
this year. The agrarian lobby’s demands are increasing.
The return from investments is declining. The credits
which are extracted are finding no clients. The subsidies

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

which have been allocated are not being realized and
even if they were realized in the planned volumes would
not change anything substantially. Appropriations and
returnable loans in their present volumes and channeled
as they are generate only a new spiral of corruption and
not the revival of agriculture.

We can no longer pay from the budget money which
gives us nothing. We now have the opportunity to choose
between imported food and our own food. Understand-
ably, our own food complex is also a guarantor of
political stability for which funds are needed. But the
question is in what volume and on what these funds are
spent.

Agrarian protectionism exists throughout the civilized
world. Japan, India, and the United States sometimes
restrict the import and export of food to maintain and
preserve their own agricultures. The majority of devel-
oped countries use subsidies methods to observe their
own agrarian policy. How are state funds channeled and
on what do they go here? Their methods are completely
different from ours. Here it is not a question of the
mentality or specific features of a people and their
economy.

We also have peasants and farms, of various forms of
ownership, which could produce an effect with state
support. Including those peasants whom we have often
described in the newspaper.

Viktor Chumak, a private farmer from Penza Oblast, for
instance, has long lost hope with regard to the state as a
financial partner and he has found foreign sponsors for a
joint project to develop his farm, preferring to trade
himself in his food on the market to contacts with
processing. And so he is not merely keeping afloat but
flourishing.

Viktor Gulov in Kaluga Oblast has undertaken, on a
collective farm, a gradual transition to private ownership
relations and as a result has increased yield several times
over and preserved the peasants’ cultural and social life
and social protection.

Of course, both Chumak and Gulov would need support
from the state. The former for partnership, the latter to
create a testing ground for new forms of transition to the
market. In our countryside there are not that many
people and establishments of this kind for state financial
investment producing an obvious effect. And it would
not be so disadvantageous for us citizens as the present
expenditure on the countryside. And we would rapidly
receive the effect in the form of food.

But there are no such funds in the structure of state
support for the countryside. And the numerous items of
expenditure on agriculture are either unpromising or
known to be ineffective. Some crumbs from this
financing may be snatched by semibankrupt or com-
pletely bankrupt collective farms and debt-ridden pri-
vate farmers merely to avoid dying today and to prolong
the agony.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

According to the calculations and forecasts of specialists
who took part in our investigation, the funds which are
being spent today on the countryside to virtually no
purpose could produce a real return if they were chan-
neled toward priority avenues defined by state policy:
primarily the privately owned sector of economic man-
agement, pedigree livestock breeding, and elite seed
growing, efficient agricultural enterprises, interfarm
sharing, and so forth. Mistakes are possible in deter-
mining priorities. But that is not so important or detri-
mental. The main thing is that the state should have
priorities in agrarian policy and that the modest funds
which we can earmark for agriculture should be directed
where they can provide a return and should not spread
out thinly for all the big eaters as is done now.

The IZVESTIYA analysis center, the institute of eco-
nomic problems of the transitional period, the
“SOVEKON” analytical service, editorial office staffers
V. Konovalov arid Ye. Yakovieva, and the newspaper's
own correspondents Yu. Bespalov, A. Pushkar, A. Yer-
shov, M. Ovcharov, S. Krayukhin, A. Aderekhin, A.
Kislov, and V. Filippov took part in the preparation of
these items.

Munitions Institute’s Funding Cut by ‘85-90
Percent’

MM2911162394 Moscow Russian Television Network
in Russian 0640 GMT 24 Nov 94

[From the “Military Courier” program: Report over
video of Moscow Machine Building Research institute]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] [passage omitted]

[Correspondent] The Moscow Machine Building Scien-
tific Research Institute dates back to 1932. This civilian
facade hides a unique, top-secret complex. Over the 60
years or so of its existence, more than 1,000 types of
munitions and missile warheads have been developed
within the institute’s walls. What you see are prototypes
of charges, many of which are without parallel in the
world. The complexity of the institute’s work is illus-
trated by the fact that it requires the collaboration of
more than 600 of the most varied types of enterprises.
There are only six countries in the world where the entire
munitions production process from development to
mass production takes place. For the time being Russia
still features among the six.

While you are watching this report, elections to the
recently established Academy of Artillery and Missile
Sciences are taking place in Moscow. The most out-
standing scientists in this field are uniting to try and save
a sphere in which they were once world leaders.

{Academician Vladimir Kireyev, institute’s general
designer, identified by caption] The problem is not just
funding, although this is frequently mentioned. Our
funding has decreased substantially, by some 85-90
percent. It is also the controllability of the complex.
Whereas for a relatively long time we had virtual parity
with European countries...aproximately 70 percent of
our technology was up to world-class standard, in some
areas we were ahead, and in others we were slightly
lagging. At the moment we are just managing to hang on,
but in three to five years we will have fallen behind.
