‘Full Text’ of Yeltsin Decree on Ostankino TV

LD3011114094 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 0859 GMT 30 Nov 94

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 30 Nov (ITAR-
TASS)—President Boris Yeltsin has issued a decree “On
Improving the Use of Channel One (Moscow) Television
and its Distribution Network.” Here follows the full text
of the document circulated today by the head of state’s
press service.

“For the purposes of developing independent television
broadcasting and ensuring guarantees of freedom of
speech and mass information, I resolve:

1. To accept the proposal by the Russian state television
and radio company Ostankino, agreed with the Russian
Federal Television and Radio Broadcasting Service, on
this television and radio company joining the “Public
Russian Television” joint-stock company as a
cofounder, with 51 percent of shares under federal
ownership.

2. To establish that from the moment of its foundation
the “Public Russian Television” joint-stock company
will broadcast on Channel One (Moscow) television and
also on the Moscow-Global satellite television system.

3. To extend to the “Public Russian Television” joint-
stock company the legal management framework estab-
lished for the All-Russian State Television and Radio
Broadcasting Company [Russian Television]. Payment
of services provided to the “Public Russian Television”

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

joint-stock company by state communications enter-
prises and the television technical center are to be made
according to rates set for state television and radio
broadcasting companies.

4. The Government of the Russian Federation is
instructed:

- to ensure within a month from the day the “Public
Russian Television” joint-stock company is founded
that it is issued with broadcasting licenses and licenses
for communications activity in the field of television and
radio broadcasting in accordance with established pro-
cedures;

- to ensure transmissions from 1 February 1995 of
programs of the “Public Russian Television” joint-stock
company on Channel One (Moscow) television, and also
on the Moscow-Global satellite television system, while
maintaining existing conditions for the distribution of
programs;

- within a month from the day the “Public Russian
Television” joint-stock company is founded, to adopt a
decision on the legal management framework, estab-
lished in point three above, on issues within the compe-
tence of the Government of the Russian Federation.

5. This decree comes into force on the day it is signed.“
Regional Affairs

Sverdlovsk Charter Approved With Yeltsin Aid

MK3011112094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 30 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Viktor Smirnov: “Authorities Conflict in
Yekaterinburg. Speaker Defeats Governor With Presi-
dent’s Help”’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The duel between Governor
Aleksey Strakhov and Sverdlovsk Oblast Duma Speaker
Eduard Rossel has ended in complete victory for the
more experienced Rossel: The governor’s resistance was
rendered useless after Boris Yeltsin backed the draft
charter the Duma elaborated for the oblast thanks to the
speaker’s persistence. In yesterday’s news briefing in
Yekaterinburg, Aleksey Strakhov told about the final
version of the charter approved by the Duma 25
November.

Differences between the heads of the local power
branches on the question of a charter for Sverdlovsk
Oblast came as a continuation of the high-profile scandal
surrounding the creation of a Urals Republic. Eduard
Rossel, the author of the idea, who had lost the position
of governor in a direct conflict with Moscow, decided to
try a different tack and implement his republic dream as
oblast Duma speaker. Aleksey Strakhov, new head of the
administration, thought it his duty to oppose the
attempt. Therefore the governor declined to sign the
charter drafted by the deputies, pleading that the presi-
dent’s staff experts found some of the articles in the
document out of keeping with the Constitution.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

The conflict between the governor and the speaker
threatened to develop into a protracted standoff, but
Eduard Rossel once again resorted to a flanking move-
ment. Elbowing his way into Yeltsin’s reception room,
the speaker not only convinced him that the draft charter
was fully in line with the Constitution, but also secured
the president’s consent to the holding of elections for
governor in the oblast. Rossel consolidated his victory
first at the sitting of the conciliation commission
attended by Moscow and local specialists where a new
version of the charter was hammered out (which
retained, in the view of local observers, all the funda-
mental provisions of the previous draft), and then in a
special sitting of the oblast Duma where the charter was
approved by an overwhelming majority of votes.

Thus, the battle around the charter may be considered at
an end; the governor would hardly go against the will of
the president and veto the document once again. True,
for the speaker this may prove to be a Pyrrhic victory.
Should Eduard Rossel be elected governor in spring, the
broad powers of deputies that the charter provides
for—in particular the right to confirm the chairman of
the oblast government and heads of the oblast adminis-
tration departments appointed by head of the adminis-
tration—may prove quite inopportune for him.

Security Stepped Up on Stavropol Border With
Chechnya

LD3011094394 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0900
GMT 30 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Nikolay Styazhkin]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Stavropol November 30
TASS—The situation in the southern Russian Stavropol
region bordering on Chechnya remained quiet during the
first 24 hours of the ultimatum of the Russian president
issued to the rebel Republic of Chechnya.

However, the local administration ordered police to
mount border control. ““The task is to rule out the spread
of weapons from Chechnya’’, spokesman of the
Stavropol administration Valeriy Oliyanchuk told TASS
on Wednesday [30 November].

He added that no increase in the number of Chechen
refugees, no extremist or nationalist actions were regis-
tered in the region.

No concentration of Russian troops has been reported in
the region either, TASS was told by competent sources.
The units which were transferred here from Transcau-
casia two years ago remain in barracks and are engaged
in routine training, the sources said.

On Tuesday President Boris Yeltsin ordered the warring
sides in Chechnya to lay down arms in 48 hours threat-
ening otherwise to impose a state of emergency on the
rebel region. The ultimatum expires early on Thursday.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 35

Experts Say Komi Oil Spill Clean-Up Too Slow

LD2811190994 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1609
GMT 28 Nov 94

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] The clean up operation and
construction of protecting installations proceed too
slowly near Usinsk, Komi Republic, where a Komineft
oil pipeline broke down last August and September. This
conclusion was made by a team of Fuel and Energy
Ministry, Ministry for Emergencies, Nature Protection
Ministry and Water Installation Committee experts who
stayed on the site on November 22-25, officials in the
Ministry for Emergencies press service reported
Monday.

According to the experts, as little as 4.7 hectares of the
forest tundra have been cleared up and 2200 cubic
meters of oil, or just over 8 percent of the pollution,
removed. A total of over 26.4 hectares had been pol-
luted.

The experts are also worried about the stamina of the
affected part of the pipeline.

Out of the 52 kilometers of a new pipeline to be laid in
the firs: half of December just over one half have been
welded, insulated and put in place. A state custom duty
of 2.5 * Vion rubles has to be paid for the pipes that were
brought vw Usinsk but the oil company does not have this
amount, the Ministry for Emergencics reports.

According to Komineft, shortage of funds handicaps the
clean up operation. The probability is high that the
operation will not be complete before the spring floods.

The spread of the oil leak is now kept in check by 30
degrees below zero Celsius frost. If, however, the clean
up is not complete before the spring, the oil is bound to
reach the Barents Sea.

Komineft managers believe that at least 14,000 tonnes of
crude leaked. The Russian-American Nordeco ecological
joint venture says that, according to air photographs,
103,000 tonnes have leaked and some oil has reached the
Pechora River which flows into the Barents Sea. Western
sources say that the Komi disaster is the largest since the
break up of the Exxon Valdez oil tanker off the Alaskan
coast in 1989.

Dangers of N. Caucasus Emergency Status Viewed

MM2911124694 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 26 Nov 94 First Edition p 2

[Article by Sergey Karkhanin: “Fire Will Not Smolder
Long. Can State of Emergency Bring Peace to Ossetian-
Ingush Conflict Zone?”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] The first 10 days of December
will be full of important events for the North Caucasus.
The state of emergency expires at the start of the month
and the question of whether to extend it—as is well
known, it has already been in force for three years—will
arise. The situation in the conflict zone will then be
discussed by the Federation Council, and, judging by
past debates in the upper house of parliament on this
topic, none of the possible plans will have an easy time
when it comes to a vote. And, finally, the Congress of
Repressed Peoples will express its position 9 December
on the refugee problem—the most urgent and explosive
of all the problems in the region. However the debate
may end, one thing is clear—the tactic of postponing
matters has virtually run its course.

The recent armed clash in Nazran between outside
Special-Purpose Militia Detachment [OMON] men and
townspeople confirmed that the ashes of the dying fire
contain sparks capable of reviving a huge conflagration.
The Interim Administration’s version of events is
already well known, but there is also another version—
one put forward by Ruslan Aushev. It boils down to
saying that tr> OMON, without warning the local
authorities, attacked an apartment block that was home
to defenseless elderly people, opened fire without the
slightest provocation, and then took several entirely
innocent people hostage. This version will not sound
improbable to anyone familiar with the situation
reigning in the conflict zone and adjoining regions....

Deputies in both houses of the federal parliament are
increasingly often criticizing the Interim Administra-
tion, and with good cause. Over the past two years only
37 families have been able to return to the homes they
left during the fighting in Prigorodnyy Rayon. The
striking figure of 12,000 people that features in a number
of reports is a fiction—the authors of the reports include
among the “returnees” those who never went anywhere
(as was the case in the settlement of Mayskiy, where
Ossets and Ingush did not fight each other and continue
to live in peace and harmony, thereby refuting the widely
held extremist slogan that it is “impossible” for the two
peoples to “live together’).

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

But many homes in the zone have been burned down and
destroyed; they will have to be urgently restored. The
Russian Government has allocated funds, but the local
authorities are in no hurry to use them. Over the past two
months construction has begun on only 19 apartment
blocks. At that rate the refugees will barely be back by
early next century....

It would be naive to believe that the refugees are being
prevented from returning by the hostility of neighbors of
a different ethnic group. Here is a recent example from
the settlement of Chermen—a returning woman was
given a very friendly welcome by her neighbors, but then
busloads of aggressive people appeared out of nowhere
and carnage began.... It cannot be ruled out that a mobile
organized group of extremist provocateurs is operating
in the zone and that its task is to come up with all kinds
of obstacles to reconciliation.

Nor can we get away from the fact that just next door is
the politically seething area of Chechnya. But the cliche
view that events in Chechnya are hampering a settlement
of the Ossetian-Ingush conflict is not necessarily true.
Here is what Magomed-Bashir Abadiyev, a member of
the Congress of Ethnic Associations of Russia, who
visited the North Caucasus the other day, thinks on this
score:

“T believe that the two elements of the generally accepted
formula should change places. Until the constitutional
rights of refugees are restored in the zone, Chechnya will
not change its policy of state autonomy. Chechens do not
want to repeat the sad experience of the Ingush and
Ossets—Chechnya is simply scared of Russia. That is
why Groznyy is seeking and establishing contact with the
Baltic and certain other states—and on the basis of
anti-Russian sentiment. Incidentally, we should not dis-
count the interest that a number of Asian and Near East
states have in cutting Chechnya off from the unified
Russian oil refining complex. Capital and weapons are
being used to this end, and—if necessary—‘black
legions’ sent in from afar will get involved on Dudayev’s
side. Bringing the center of fierce confrontation as close
to Russia as possible is the cherished dream of those
foreign politicians who have set themselves the goal of
triggering a chain reaction of collapse in the North
Caucasus.”

So the problem of stability in the state-of-emergency
zone is acquiring new political momentum. Incidentally
this does not prevent mysterious armored personnel
carriers from criss-crossing Sunzhenskiy Rayon with
impunity—although neither Groznyy, Vladikavkaz, nor
the Interim Administration agree to recognize them as
their own. The deaths of several “persons of a non-
Caucasian ethnic group” in a battle near Urus-Martan
provide new food for conjecture.

“Criminals have now become national heroes,” Vasiliy
Tarasenko, chairman of the Federation Council Com-
mittee for Federation Affairs, the Federation Treaty, and

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

Regional Policy, believes. ““The zone is being milita-
rized. In general, neither the federal nor the regional
authorities are ready to lift the state of emergency.”

Nonetheless, it is clearly too dangerous to extend the
state of emergency indefinitely, relying on it alone. “Will
only the dead return?” asked a sorrowful elderly lady
from a family of refugees for whom the few kilometers to
her own home has been an insuperable distance for two
years now. What answer will she get from the Russian
lawmakers when the debates on the problems of the
North Caucasus resume? Around 50,000 people are
awaiting a decision on their fate.

leaders of the North Caucasian region and the Federal
Assembly’s deputies representing the North Caucasian

resolute measures to prevent the escalation of the Rus-
sian troops’ presence in Chechnya.”

Communist Leader on State of Emergency in
Chechnya

LD2911143594 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1404
GMT 29 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS parliamentary correspondent Pavel
Kuznetsov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 29 TASS—
The state of emergency is the last step “that nay entail
most grave consequences if made without consultations
and negotiations of the presidential and government
teams with all parties to the conflict,” Russian commu-
nist leader Gennadiy Zyuganov told ITAR-TASS today.

Commenting on the statement of the Russian president,
Zyuganov said that Chechnya has long lived on its own
laws which resulted in a civil war. “The Russian presi-
dent and the government should have timely reacted to
these facts,” he added.

The Russian Communisi Party stands for “using all
political methods, personal meetings and negotiations
with any side in the Chechen Republic in order to agree
on termination of bloodshed,” said Zyuganov.

An immediate meeting of the Federation Council is
necessary in addition to a Duma meeting to discuss the
Chechen situation, he said.

The communist leader called on the warring sides in
Chechnya to abstain from lynching POWs or political
opponents “since this will result in revenge and
increasing mutual dislike, on one hand, and fail to settle
a problem, on the other hand.”

fighting in Groznyy on 26 November are being verified.

killed on both sides and about 80 are missing. The
peaceful population regards the events that have hap-
pened as a national tragedy. On 29 November both

Council’s united forces, recording] The statement came
as a surprise, especially at a moment when we were
getting ready to storm the town once again. But I think
that in order to avoid further bloodshed, we will suspend
military actions, and in this way Yeltsin’s instructions
will play the decisive role. [end recording]

[Begin O.Satuyev, fighter, recording] I am fed up with
this life. I want to return to peaceful life, to put on a suit,
to shave myself, to walk about the town. I am fed up with
fighting. [end recording]

On the border with Chechnya, Russian servicemen con-
tinue to work as usual and carry out border check-ups.
According to officers, they have not received any orders
linked to the Russian president’s statement.

Dudayev Condemns Yeltsin Statement, Bombing

LD2911163994 Moscow Mayak Radio Network in
Russian 1500 GMT 29 Nov 94

{[FBIS Translated Text] [Announcer] Our correspondent
Anton Zharov has managed to get communicate once

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 41

again with Groznyy and he held a conversation with
Chechnya’s President Dzhokhar Dudayev. He asked him
to give his assessment of the situation that has arisen in
the republic after Boris Yeltsin’s statement. Now listen
to their conversation:

[Begin Dudayev recording] The people regards the state-
ment itself as yet another act of violence, intimidation,
and blackmail. The whole people has risen up—all the
citizens of the republic regardless of nationality or ethnic
group. The square in Groznyy is filled with the whole
civilian population demanding the end of aggression.
Otherwise it may lead to a situation in which those who
will suffer most are people of non-Chechen origin; and
they are already suffering.

At 1530, while the news conference was being held in the
presence of over 20 representatives of the foreign press,
the town of Groznyy was directly, without a twinge of
conscience, fired upon; men and beasts, people, citizens,
everything that was in the way just fell.

This statement showed where, according to the appeal of
the people and the citizens...[pauses] Thank you, Boris
Yeltsin. Now the people have learned the source of the
threats of aggression and violence.

Everything that was said in that statement has happened
precisely as Boris Nikolayev predicted. Combat actions
here have ceased. Except that the Russian Air Force is
making strikes against the undefended town and the
peaceful population.

The opponents have laid down their weapons, every
single one. The armored equipment has been completely
surrendered. The constitutional structure has been
restored. The government is fully in control of the
situation and is engaged in restoring the national
economy.

Thus, the grounds for disquiet expressed in Boris
Yeltsin’s statement—that this needs to be done and that
needs to be done—are totally lacking both for the Rus-
sian leadership and the Russian people. The equipment
has been seized from the interventionists and they have
been disarmed. They are all under arrest.

Government commissions and governmental brigades,
state committees that have been set up have been set up
again. Lawyers are busy with juridical questions of the
legality of the violations. Economists are engaged in
estimating the material and economic damage. Produc-
tion experts are engaged in restoring production. In
general the government and the citizens should get on
with their usual business.

The people are holding rallies. Representatives of all
peoples, all nationalities have been coming here since the
summer from all ends of the earth. There are reports that
people are coming here from Near Eastern countries. I
have just received reports that they are even going to
fight their way through if anyone opposes them.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

a RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

No talks at all are required here. In 1992 Russia set out
its national concept and state policy in the Caucasus, in
which it included Chechnya, Dagestan, and Azerbaijan
among its potential enemies. From this stems the polit-
ical program of those who carry it out—the special
services and the hawks. None of them intends to go in for
talks. They all want secret talks to try to persuade us.
Some one comes here and says: Let us whisper together.
Let us have a little talk. But we say: Come officially with
some document showing that you are empowered to hold
talks. There have been so many of these secret talks. But
it is necessary to hold talks with someone who bears
responsibility for the results of the talks and there has
been no sign of anyone like that in Russia yet. [end
recording]

[Announcer] That was a statement by Chechen President
Dzhokhar Dudayev. People of non-Chechen origin are
suffering. That report makes me very worried. But the
tone of the statement, as you heard, is optimistic, disci-
plined, and confident.

Moscow Set To Use Military Force in Chechnya

MK3011105094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 30 Nov 94 p 1

[Report by Dmitriy Kamyshev under the “Situation in
Chechnya” rubric: “Boris Yeltsin Gives Two Days for
Reilection”’]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] [Passage omitted] Although
there have been repeated instances in modern Russian

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

history where the president softened the tone cand
meaning of his harsh statements, yesterday’s statement
by Yeltsin is unlikely to share this fate. First, many
influential Russian politicians have already spoken up in
support of tough measures; second, both warring fac-
tions are prodding Moscow to resolve the Chechen
conflict by force: the opposition by its inability to
overpower Dudayev, and the Chechen authorities by
their stubborn unwillingness to part with the idea of
Chechen independence. [passage omitted]

Meanwhile, the very possibility of Moscow’s peaceful
steps in this situation raises serious doubts. In fact, a
peaceful solution to the conflict is possible only in two
cases: if the Russian leadership puts up with Chechnya’s
secession and switches its relations with it to an inter-
state mode, or if Dzhokhar Dudayev gives up indepen-
dence and complies with Yeltsin’s ultimatum. Since the
likelihood of both these developments is infinitely low,
statements by Security Council members on the peaceful
nature of measures under discussion should apparently
be interpreted as a tactical ploy aimed at deceiving the
enemy. The utmost seriousness of the decisions dis-
cussed was indirectly confirmed by the fact that right
after the Security Council meeting, Prime Minister
Viktor Chernomyrdin canceled his trip to Novosibirsk
scheduled for ! December.

The main debate during the meeting appears to have
focused on the procedure and scale of forcible actions to
be taken in Chechnya. If the situation is to be taken
under control, the Kremlin can now resort only to two
scenarios: a “classical” scenario involving a gradual
redeployment of internal troops units into the republic to
assume control over post offices, bridges, telegraph com-
munications, and so forth; and an “Afghan” scenario
including the airlifting of special-purpose units to
Groznyy, a swift assault on the presidential palace, and
the rescue of hostages. The first method is less risky, but
Moscow already has a bitter experience of applying it in
Chechnya three years ago. The second option was suc-
cessfully tested in the countries of “‘people’s democracy”
in the past, but it may involve considerable casualties.

The president will apparently make a definitive decision
on the use of military force, taking into account stances
by chiefs of the power agencies: They stand to benefit
most from this solution to the “Chechen problem” if it
proves successful, but they will turn out to be scapegoats
in case of failure. And if, for instance, Pavel Grachev
wishes to prove that he is actually the best defense
minister in the past several years, the president, to all
appearances, will give him an opportunity to do so.

MVD Troops Chief: Chechnya Terrorism Possible

LD2911153894 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1504 GMT 29 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Yelena Gorelova]

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 29 Nov—Colonel Gen-
eral Anatoliy Kulikov, commander of the Russian
internal troops, does not rule out the possibility of
terrorist acts being perpetrated by Chechen fighters. He
is particularly concerned in this connection about
nuclear power stations and other strategic targets. The
Col. Gen. said at an itinerant briefing at the main
directorate of the commander of the interior troops of
the Russian Ministry of Internal Affairs [MVD] that of
late there had been 10 anonymous telephone calls at
Russian nuclear power stations about bombs being
planted, although in each case the information had not
been confirmed.

According to Kutikov, the internal troops had stepped
up their guarding of strategic targets since the exacerba-
tion of the situation in Chechnya.

The commander also stressed that it is within our power
to alter the situation in zones of protracted conflicts, thus
preventing a repetition of a Chechnya-type development
of events.

Dudayev: Emergency Would Mean Caucasus War
MM2911202794 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 30 Nov 94 pp I, 2

[Report by special correspondent Aleksandr Yevtush-
enko incorporating interview with President Dzhokhar

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

44 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Dudayev in Groznyy; date not given: “Women and
Children Evacuated From Groznyy”]}

[FBIS Translated Text] Yesterday in the second half of
the day official Groznyy reacted to Boris Yeltsin’s ulti-
matum by declaring a general evacuation and preparing
for the state of emergency. Vehicles carrying weapons to
be issued to volunteer militia members were sent to the
city center. All communication with the outside world is
being cut off.

The last shots had barely ceased when workers’ detach-
ments appeared in Groznyy. They came not to demon-
strate their support or provest but to work. Streetcar and
trolleybus lines destroyed by tanks are rapidly being
restored. Windows and store windows blown out by
explosions are being g:azed. This labor blitz might have
seemed strange to many city inhabitants had an emer-
gency government session chaired by Dzhokhar
Dudayev not been broadcast on local television the
previous day. During it he literally demanded that his
ministers channel all manpower and resources into the
fastest possible restoration of everything destroyed
during the assault. It was at this point that the Chechen
president began our conversation.

[Dudayev] The main brunt of the bandit formations’
strike was borne by peaceful inhabitants, their homes,
and city utilities. The city was left without transport
communications, and in places electricity was cut off
and water supplies were affected. The most immediate
and the main task is to restore all this. They also fired at
the government palace, at my office windows. But more
as a warning. When the assault began I made a diver-
sionary maneuver: I sent my car from the palace toward
my home. The bandits decided that I was not here and
started using their guns to shell the state security depart-
ment and the MVD [Ministry of Internal Affairs].
During all this time I was at my workplace. Although it
cannot be said that I directed the defense. Such a bold
breakthrough by the enemy took us by surprise. There-
fore there was no time left to properly organize resis-
tance. The city’s only defense coordination center was
operating. Which, incidentally, is not defended by any-
thing. Therefore what the resistance forces managed to
do was all the more surprising. I do not know of more
cases whereby two tanks were simultaneously destroyed
by one shot from a grenade launcher. Whereby in a
matter of minutes one tank destroyed five enemy
armored vehicles. There were many such examples here.

[Yevtushenko] A serious blow was undoubtedly inflicted
on the opposition forces in the Groznyy events. What is
the likelihood that they will quickly recover and make
another assault soon?

[Dudayev] If you are talking about bandit formations—
what kind of opposition is this: without a clear political
platform or civilized methods of struggle—its days are
numbered. Something else is of concern: It is not a band
of criminals that is now waging war on the people but the
leadership of a vast country—the Russian Federation.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

During the three years of our independence Russian
troops have repeatedly invaded the republic’s territory.
Now we are seeing naked aggression. Not only are the
bandits fighting with Russian weapons and for Moscow’s
money. There are also Russian Army officers in their
ranks. It is they who, having been captured, are saying:
“Give us the weapons, we will die io get our revenge on
the commanders who deceived us and pitched us into
this hell.” But only yesterday they were preparing for a
victory parade over the Chechen people. We do not
intend to kill these people. That is not our tradition.
Investigators are working with each one. The prisoners’
fate will be decided by a court. The Russian military
leadership has already abandoned its officers. Now they
are simply mercenaries.

[Yevtushenko] You once stated that other states’ soldiers
are prepared to fight on your side too....

[Dudayev] I will tell you about this right now. Volunteers
from the countries of the Arab world are increasingly
insistently requesting permission to come to Chechnya
to fight aggression. They have now moved into adjacent
states and are living in camps and waiting for the “green
light” from me. I am not talking about detachments that
have come to our aid at this time from Bulgaria,
Karachay, Adygea, and Dagestan. The second Caucasian
war has already begun. But it is increasingly threatening
to engulf the entire Caucasus. This will happen if Presi-
dent Yeltsin carries out his threats and tries to introduce
a state of emergency in Chechnya and establish a mili-
tary dictatorship regime here. [Dudayev ends]

Yes, yesterday's statement by Yeltsin caused a big stir in
Chechnya. It was probably precipitated primarily by
concern over the fate of Russian citizens who have been
taken prisoner. At any rate, before this the country’s
political leadership turned a blind eye to intra-Chechen
clashes: “Anything for a quiet life.” While the military
leadership—and this is worse—threw wood on the fire in
the form of armored vehicle and weapons deliveries to
the opposition. But the plans for a coup in Chechnya at
the hands of the Chechens themselves did not succeed,
and matters went too far.

“Undoubtedly, Dudayev has made many mistakes in his
post,” I was told yesterday by Shamil Basayev, com-
mander of the “Abkhaz” battalion, “and I would not
vote for him in the next presidential election. But he has
achieved the main thing: People believe in the idea of the
republic’s independence, and many are prepared to die
for their sovereignty and freedom. Therefore such gross
interference by Russia as the declaration of a state of
emergency is a terrible mistake. After all, we have
already gone through that, when then-Vice President
Rutskoy declared a state of emergency in the republic.
This merely united the people around Dudayev. The
same will happen now. | am sure that even most of the
remaining opposition detachments will turn their assault
rifles against the Russian soldiers.”

When this issue appears on the newsstands the Chechen
leaders will have only 24 hours left until the deadline

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

expires. I do not venture to predict what will happen
then. One thing is clear: The Chechens do not intend to
disarm.

[FBIS Translated Text] [Correspondent Petro Ostap-
enko] In beginning his meeting with representatives of
Ukrainian and foreign mass media, Leonid Kuchma
stressed the fact that the visit to the U.S. was the first

country.

[Kuchma] There were about five meetings with the U.S.
President for discussions and talks, and meetings with
Vice President Gore, State Secretary Christopher,
Defense Secretary Perry, Secretary of Commerce Brown,
Secretary of the Treasury Bentsen, and other officials of
the U.S. President’s administration.

Fourteen bilateral documents were concluded that setout
our agreement to cooperate closely in the political and
economic spheres; the peaceful exploitation of space;
power engineering, trade, and investments. I also met
with leading representatives of U.S. political forces,
senators, representatives of the Congress, famous polit-
ical analysts and civil activists. You probably know
about this from the press; therefore, I wiil not list them.
During my sojourn in New York I visited the United

should elaborate the Constitution for Ukraine and above

deputies. When there is a clear division of powers, then

should be engaged, in the legislative process, which is
vitally necessary for the state, including the elaboration
of those mechanisms on private ownership of land, so
that society does not get the impression that there is a

[Ostapenko] It is known now that many of the interna-
tional financial organizations and the world’s developed
nations are ready to provide assistance to our reforms
and are already doing so. But is there any mechanism of
the provision and use of credits?

[Kuchma] An assistance mechanism? But, of course, it
has been primed and is in place as the mechanism that is
needed. As for the International Monetary Fund’s $350
million, we have already received it. As to the nuclear
disarmament assistance, there also is a mechanism,
which is known to the specialists that are dealing with
this matter. As far as this year’s loans are concerned,
specific agreements have been signed between our
Finance Ministry and Economy Ministry and the respec-
tive ministries and departments of the United States of
America.

I, however, would only like to emphasize that my inten-
tion is to set up a single center of investment agencies,
which everything going Ukraine’s way will go through. If
anybody comes to Ukraine today and wants to invest one
dollar somewhere, they walk our lobbies of power and
that one dollar is not enough to give for somebody to
show them the door they should enter. So there will
definitely be this agency in Ukraine to deal with this
process.

On the subject of who owes whom how much, I would
like to mention that Ukraine today owes $7 billion. This
is so. I, however, would like nobody to equal us to
Russia, as our main dependence is primarily energy
carriers and the current debt for energy carriers should
be paid. As the world provided these funds for the
former Soviet Union, there was a clear knowledge that
Russia had the means of repayment, i.e. the inexhaust-
ible reserves of raw materials such as oil, gas, timber,
gold, and so on. If you affirm the opposite, saying that we
have the means of repayment, you are welcome to
(?produce it) and solve at least one problem. The
problem today is that there should be light in our house,
that it should be warm, and that our factories should
work. So the assisiance that is being rendered to us today
is first and foremost to rectify the negative balance of
payments, for us to be able to pay both Russia and
Turkmenistan primarily for gas.

[Ostapenko] The conversation returned again to the
formation in Ukraine of the vertical power structure and
the constitutional process. There were anxieties that the
Supreme Council would not be able to adopt a law on
power that the president was proposing. Supreme
Council Chairman Oleksandr Moroz did not agree with
this either. What was Leonid Kuchma’s position?

[Kuchma] I would like to remind everyone, although you
all know from reading a little history, that when a
country emerged from a difficult economic crisis it does
so only under stong executive power. Above all, if one
were to take the United States, then I would like to say
that in 1933 a special law was passed according to which
power was transferred to the U.S. President. This law

UKRAINE 51

was in effect for two years when Theodore Roosevelt
addressed the people of America on national radio
saying they had survived the difficult illness. Please tell
me, can collective bodies decide anything, especially
when there are such contradictions in society? We see
that every issue in the Supreme Council results is a
battle. Are we going to turn everything into a fight?

I would like those politicians who are putting forward
such slogans to have some responsibility before society
for this. They even gave examples on the resolution of
some specific issues. Nobody really wants to take the
responsibility on himself. They say they could repeal it
but we do not need to. I know that this process will be
very difficult. From my own point of view as an indi-
vidual, I would say it would be better for me to simply sit
it out for four years and then to say, Mr. Moroz take the
power from me and I will look at you at what you are
doing. But I am personally convinced that it will be a
{word indistinct]. If there is no master at home [word
indistinct] then it will lead to nothing good. As they say,
somebody is returning something to somebody else in
the family. Does a husband give away his wife because
she cannot cope any longer with her domestic duties?
Another way out is through a referendum. If the law on
power is not adopted, if we do not find a constitutional
agreement, then I will turn to the Supreme Council and
say let us do it this way. We will adopt a decision on a
referendum. That is all. We do not want the people to say
that the president is collecting a million signatures. If we
are really all responsible for the fate of the state, let us
adopt a decision on a referendum. Whatever the people
say will be carried out, but if we refer to the people, then
we are also deceiving rot ourselves but the people. It
depends on the information and on who shouts the
loudest. It is not just a pelitical problem; it is also an
economic problem. But nevertheless you must know that
the people are sick and tired of lawlessness. We must
urgently adopt such a decision. I ask that this law comes
into effect only until the ncw constitution is adopted,
only until ‘“en. When there is a new constitution then
this law wil. oe repealed. If we are not able to adopt such
a law within this tim2-scale, then it could be in force
until the end of the president’s or the Supreme Council’s
term. But I have already said this today that we must not
accuse one another. I have not accused anybody. But
they are saying from the TV rostrum or from some other
rostrum that the president in his decree on accelerating
land reform is violating eight articles of the constitution.

Please tell me how? This is not true. Because before
signing it I consulted first with lawyers of the Cabinet of
Ministers. The government submitted this decree. I have
some very good lawyers in the president’s administra-
tion, they are fine experts. Then we invited others to
examine this decree.

(?found anything).

As for the differences, differences in general (?abound).
Given that our constitution at present consists of

nothing but the differences, tell me please where there is

[Ostapenko] As is known, an agreement on cooperation

[Ostapenko] Journalists also raised language issues. As

begins.

Kiev Condems Attacks on Peacekeepers in Bosnia

LD2911221294 Kiev UNIAN in Ukrainian 1845 GMT
29 Nov 94

[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev—‘“Ukraine’s nermanent
mission to the United Nation has sent a note to the UN

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

secretary general, condemning the attacks on peace-
keepers in Bosnia-Herzegovina that led to the death of
eight UN personnel, including two Ukrainians, and
calling for an inquiry into the circumstances of the
attacks and prosecution of the perpetrators,” Volodymyr
Handohiy, head of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of
Ukraine international organizations directorate, told a
briefing at the ministry.

He informed journalists about Ukraine's position on
redeployment of the Ukrainian peacekeeping battalion
as the situation surrounding the Bosnian enclave of
Bihac exacerbated: Any repositioning of the Ukrainian
contingent was only possible with the Ukrainian govern-
ment’s consent.

Asked whether there were any plans to recall the Ukrai-
nian servicemen from the former Yugos!avia, Volod-
ymyr Handohiy said that this was the intention of
neither Ukraine nor any other contributing countries
and that at issue were firmer personnel security guaran-
tees. Ukraine at the same time, he added, participated
most actively in drawing up a UN convention on the
protection of peacekeepers, a draft of which will in the
very near future be under consideration by the UN
General Assembly. Ukraine insisted the draft should
include a clause on “every country’s sovereign right to
recall their forces should the situation in the combat
forces zone deteriorate in the extreme.”

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Vitaliy Makarchev]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] London November 30 TASS—
A Ukrainian crew of an armored troop carrier of the
U.N. peacekeeping forces was seized today by Bosnian
Serbs at check point No. !1 near Bihac town in Bosnia.

The seven servicemen including one officer were “taken
by force to the area close to Bosansca Krupa town,”
British General Michael Rose, representative of the
headquarters of the commander of the U.N. forces in
Bosnia-Herzegovina in Sarajevo, told ITAR-TASS.

U.N. representatives have established a contact with the
unit of Bosnian Serbs who captured Ukrainian ser-
vicemen and started negotiations with them.

"On the Political-Legal Situation in the Autonomous
Republic of Crimea“ *”]

[FBIS Translated Text] The State Duma of the Russian
Federation, understanding the full complexity of the
problems inherited by Russia and Ukraine as a result of
disintegration of the Soviet Union, recognizing the
reality of Crimea being a part of Ukraine, and prc-
ceeding from the principles of equal rights and self-
determination of peoples, sovereign equality and territo-
rial integrity of states as affixed in the Final Act of the
Conference on Security and Cooperation in Europe of
1975, states its concern with respect to the development
of mutual relations between the Supreme Council of
Ukraine and Supreme Council of the Crimean Republic.
We appeal to deputies and all citizens of Ukraine to
relate to the hopes and problems of the Crimean
Republic with tolerance and respect. The State Duma
believes that the worst way to regulate arising differences
is the language of ultimatum and blockade. It is in the
common interest of the two fraternal peoples of Russia
and Ukraine to safeguard Crimea—so oversaturated
with weapons—from any pressure from the use of force.
The State Duma is also concerned about the fate of
Crimea because any exacerbation of the situation on the
peninsula is echoed in Russia. There are no issues in the
mutual relations of Russia and Ukraine that cannot be
resolved over the course of negotiations?

Without a compromise resolution to the Crimean-
Ukrainian conflict, in our view, any agreement on the
future of the Black Sea Fleet, its moorings and bases, is
unattainable—as 1s any signing or ratification of the
Treaty on Friendship, Cooperation, and Partnership
between Russia and Ukraine. The State Duma recom-
mends that the Russian Federation Ministry of Foreign
Affairs, during the course of negotiations with the Ukrai-
nian side, in discussion of the situation that has come
about, listen to the representatives of Crimea itself. In
this regard, the State Duma affirms its readiness, already
expressed in the 25 May 1994 appeal, “to do everything
within its power to aid in the search for constructive
compromise on the basis of the existing Russian-
Ukrainian Treaty of 19 November 1990.” We call upon
the authorities of Ukraine and Crimea to exercise
restraint, show mutual respect, and take into account the
influence of decisions made on the overall background of
Russian-Ukrainian relations.

[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev [no dateline as received]—
The Supreme Council of Ukraine Presidium today sent
an Official letter via diplomatic channels to the Russian
Federation’s State Duma in connection with the latter's
statement of 23 November on Ukrainian-Crimean rela-
tions.

UKRAINE 53

The letter expresses concern at the fact that these steps
affecting Ukraine were taken ahead of Russian President
Boris Yeltsin’s state visit to Kiev, and the signing of a
large-scale political treaty.

The statement by the Russian Federation’s State Duma,
the Supreme Council of Ukraine Presidium goes on to
say, can be regarded as nothing but another act of
interfering in Ukraine’s internal affairs and grossly vio-
lating existing norms and principles of interstate rela-
tions.

The presidium’s letter points out that the positive trends
in relations between Ukraine and Russia, after Supreme
Council Chairman Oleksandr Moroz’s visit to Moscow
in June 1994 at the head of a parliamentary delegation,
have in effect been wiped out by the Russian parliamen-
tarians’s statement.

UNIAN has learned from trustworthy sources that the
wording of the initial draft of the letter from the pre-
sidium was quite harsh, underlining, in particular, that
such statements did not add to the Russian parliament's
prestige, and harmed relations between the two coun-
tries. It was, however, excluded from the final text of the
letter signed by Oleksandr Moroz.

Masol on Acceptance of ‘Zero Option,’
Repayments

LD2911132794 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1100
GMT 29 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Ukrainian Prime Minister
Vitaliy Masol stated that Ukraine is ready to accept the
so-called ‘zero option’ for settling the problem of the
former Soviet Union’s assets and liabilities.

He told Interfax, after talks with Russian Prime Minister
Viktor Chernomyrdin in Moscow on Monday, that just a
few minor issues remain to be coordinated.

The ‘zero option’ implies Kiev’s pledge to withdraw its
claims from the former Soviet Union’s assets in return
for Russia servicing the Ukrainian part of the former
Soviet Union’s foreign debt.

To quote Masol, a protocol on the schedule for discus-
sion and conclusion of the key bilateral documents has
been signed in Moscow. These documents deal with the
timetable for the repayment of the Ukrainian energy,
fuel and commodity supply debt to Russia—climbing to
$635 million in 1994—the restructuring of Kiev’s debts
to Moscow, and the problems of the Black Sea Fleet.

Earlier, a source in the Russian delegation told Interfax
that the Ukrainian and Russian premiers had instructed
the leaders of the state delegations to sum up the results
of the talks and consultations on all of the above-
mentioned issues, and present a report on draft decisions
by December 20.

This source also said that judging from this schedule,
President Yeltsin’s official visit to Kiev, during which a

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

84 UKRAINE

comprehensive bilateral treaty is expected to be signed
and cardinal agreements on key bilateral relation prob-
lems concluded, is unlikely to take place this year. It is
likely to be put off until the beginning of the next year, he
said.

Maso! also told Interfax that the date of President
Yeltsin’s visit to Kiev had not been discussed during the
talks. It will be fixed after the sides have drafted bilateral
agreements and a large- scale political treaty, he said.

Russia’s Vice Premier Aleksey Bolshakov, who took part
in the talks, said that a contract had been prepared on
further gas supplies to Ukraine in December 1994. He
also said that an agreement was being drafted on gas
supplies in 1995.

The protocol on gas supplies for December 1994 is likely
to be signed on Tuesday, he added.

Bolshakov also said that Ukraine as of late has been
making regular payments for gas, in accordance with the
protocol, although Kiev’s debt is still large.

“Russia cannot supply gas to Ukraine for free. I’m
convinced, however, that a specific form of payments
will be found,” he said.

“Russia and Ukraine must belong to a single economic
system” said Masol.

Belarus

Lukashenka Convinces Deputies on Election
Decree

LD3011123994 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1048
GMT 30 Nov 94

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] On Tuesday [29 November]
Belarusian parliament adopted a decree on people’s
deputies elections on a majority basis.

Our Interfax correspondent reports that President Aly-
aksandr Lukashenka persisted in adopting this very
variant. He declared in the parliament that if the depu-
ties pass a different voting system he would refuse to
sign.

The elections will be held in one-mandate electoral
districts on the basis of an equal and direct election right
with voting being secret.

The decree stipulates that supreme bodies of political
parties and public organizations, working staffs, staffs of
secondary and higher schools, meetings of electors at the
places of their residence, military units and detachments
can nominate their candidates to the people’s deputies.

Electoral blocs can nominate candidates who are mem-
bers of political parties making up the electoral bloc.

The state takes on all expenditures related to the prepa-
ration and holding of elections. The Republic’s Supreme
Soviet must confirm these expenditures.

The number of electoral districts in Belarus has been
reduced from 310 to 260.

Chairman of the Central Election Commission
Aleksandr Abramovich declared to Interfax that due to
the fact that the mandates of the present composition of
the Supreme Soviet are to expire on March 4, 1995,
parliamentarians should determine the date for future
elections, which was not confirmed on Tuesday.

Lukashenka on Status of Economic Policy

WS3011124194 Minsk ZVYAZDA in Belarusian 29
Nov 94 p 1

[“Text” of statement oy Belarusian President Alyak-
sandr Lukashenka on economic policy; place and date
not given]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Since it assumed power the
leadership of our country has drafted and been stead-
fastly carrying out the Program for Urgent Measures for
Overcoming the Economic Crisis. In a short period of
time, foreign trade and prices for the majority of goods
and services, except for several items such as bread,
milk, and municipal services, have been liberalized;
budget subsidies for them have been abolished; manage-
ability over the budget has been restored (during the first
twenty days of November, the budget revenues increased
by 179 percent, and the budget expenditures increased

WESTERN REGION 55

by only 102 percent, compared with the corresponding
period last year; credits for budget deficit were sharply
reduced—given the 170 billion rubles [R] approved for
these credits, as little as R50 billion was granted); the
Belarusian ruble has become our national currency; the
National Bank’s bank rate has approached the market
interest rate; current deliveries of energy sources from
Russia have practically been paid in full; our debt to
Western creditors has decreased; the accumulation of
debts has been halted; the financial discipline of compa-
nies has been normalized by curbing credits and subsi-
dies; strict measures for saving energy have been intro-
duced.

One must also admit, however, that while reforming the
economy, delays in the establishment of a market and
competitive environment, privatization and demonopo-
lization of the production sector, further liberalization of
foreign trade, and the implementation of the mechanism
of bankruptcy have taken place.

Liberalization of the economy should be accompanied
by appropriate measures for social protection of the
peopie. Due to the fact that liberalization of prices was
not coordinated with the introduction of measures for
social protection of the poorest segments of society in the
beginning of November, the balance between increases
in prices and citizens’ incomes was dramatically dis-
rupted. By force of his power the president had to make
a decision to introduce regulated prices for certain
foodstuffs and support layers of society which suffered
most from this.

In terms of strategy, this by no means stands for a
rejection of the principle of free pricing as a market
basis, on which reforms are being conducted in our
country. The Cabinet of Ministers has been charged with
introducing measures for social protection targeted
exclusively at the poorest layers of society, in the form of
targeted payments and benefits. This will make it pos-
sible to return to our consistent policy of liberalizing
prices in accordance with the previously adopted pro-
gram as early as December of 1994.

To overcome delays in separate directions of economic
reforms, I have given the Cabinet of Ministers appro-
priate commissions that wiil make it possible to accel-
erate privatization and the transformation of enterprises
into joint stock companies; to provide real financial
support to entrepreneurship and to introduce the prin-
ciple of establishing private production companies; to
stop interfering in pricing processes, except for products
and services of natural monopolies; to reduce subsidies
for municipal services; to create a budget system, which
will ease taxation pressure on the sectors of production
and services, stimulate their development, and reduce
inefficient budget expenditures; to reduce the deficit of
payments by stimulating export, reducing import,
removing limits in foreign trade, establishing a full-
fledged market for hard currencies, and attracting for-
eign investors.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

56 WESTERN REGION

The aforementioned methods of carrying out economic
reforms represent strategic directions for the Republic’s
development, which should later be presented in the
form of a mid-term economic program of the president
and the government.

The president and the government, while making all
essential efforts aimed at establishing an entire system of
legal and organizational assistance to the Program for
Urgent Measures for Overcoming the Economic Crisis in
the framework of constitutional requirements, are con-
vinced that all layers of the Belarusian people, political
parties, and public movements are supporting their
practical work. We also count on the help of the world’s
economic community, its financial institutions, the gov-
ernments of developed countries, and foreign business
circles in achieving the goals we set.

Hryb Returns From Poland, Notes ‘Cooperation’

WS2911130294 Minsk BELAPAN in English 1600
GMT 28 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Upon his return from Poland,
Chairman of the Supreme Soviet of Belarus Myacheslaw
Hryb and other members of the Belarusian parliamen-
tary delegation held a press conference on the results of
the trip. In the course of the three-day official visit paid
on the invitation of Jozef Oleksy, chairman of the Polish
Sejm, the delegation intended to become acquainted
with the Polish parliamentarians’ activity and the mech-
anism of interaction of power branches, as well as to
establish closer contacts between the parliaments of the
two countries and reveal reasons hampering the devel-
opment of cooperation.

Mr. Hryb stressed that the countries concluded over two
dozens of bilateral agreements, but almost all of them
did not work at all or worked extremely inefficiently. For
instance, the agreement on establishing an interstate
bank was signed three years ago, but things are not
moving, and payments are carried out via Washington or
New York. Meanwhile, the cooperation between the two
states has every prospect of success. That is especially
important taking into account that Belarus stands on the
initial stage of market reforms, whereas its western
neighbor has already got over it. This means that Belarus
has a lot to learn from Poland. This year in Poland,
production is expected to increase 10 percent against last
year, and average monthly wage stands at about $240.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

The delegation held talks with President Lech Walesa, as
well as Jozef Oleksy, chairman of the Sejm; Adam
Struzik, chairman of the Senate; Prime Minister Wal-
demar Pawlak; met with representatives of the Polish
Ministry of Foreign Affairs and parliamentary commit-
tees. At present, there are about 260 public and political
organizations and movements in Poland, but only a sixth
part of them are represented at the parliament. Every
parliamentary faction expressed willingness to meet with
the visitors from Belarus.

At the press conference, Myacheslaw Hryb stressed that
in spite of the fact that political factions at both the Sejm
and Senate had a lot of differences, all of them, never-
theless, share one main thing—support to the general
strategy of home and foreign policy. Volha Halubovich,
deputy chairman of the Supreme Soviet’s committee for
family and youth affairs, who also went to Poland,
declared in an interview to a BELAPAN correspondent
that the Polish colleagues had displayed a sincere con-
cern about both Belarus’ problems and the development
of bilateral relations.

Lech Walesa said openly: “I would not like to see tanks
on the move near our eastern border.” This statement is
quite in line with the principle of neutrality declared by
Belarus. “‘In order to make our state really independent
both politically and economically, Mrs. Halubovich
emphasized, ’we should use the willingness of Poland
and other countries of the region for cooperation.“
Speaking about ways of cooperation, she cited the Polish
president who said: ’The West is highly prompt. We
must be prompt twice as much in order not to become an
object of exploitation.

Former Defense Minister on Current Issues

WS$2911130194 Minsk BELAPAN in English GMT
1600 28 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] “Lukashenka has no right to be
leader of the nation”. With this statement, Pavel
Kazlowski, former Belarusian defense minister, began
his press conference on 28 November. He stressed that
he was uneasy about the current developments taking
place in the Belarusian Army. “Only in the last four-five
months,” he said, “over 3,000 officers—people who
promoted reforms in the Armed Forces—left the ser-
vice”. The former defense minister dwelt at length on the
rumors about selling an anti-missile missile plant S-
300PMU1 to the American-Canadian A.T.O.S. Com-
pany. According to him, the BELTECHEXPORT com-
pany started an active agitation for selling this unique
plant as far back as autumn last year. However, the
former defense minister was adamantly opposed to this
‘because we should have done damage to defense capa-
bility”. Pavel Kazlowski gave warnings of this twice to
Prime Minister Kebich. Nevertheless, this year
BELATECHEXPORT and A.T.O.S. signed a contract
on selling the plant. “I view this as a crime”, Mr.
Kazlowski said.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

The former minister of defense expressed his perplexity
about a statement by Alyaksandr Lukashenka at a
meeting with trade union leaders where he declared: “In
the Army, there are 171,000 ‘bayonets.’ “The problem
does not lie in the fact that the president cited this
figure,” he said, “the essence of the matter is that the
commander in chief of the Belarusian Army (the presi-
dent) does not know precisely what is under his com-
mand.” According to Mr. Kazlowski, the Belarusian
Army numbers about 40,000 people of serving staff. But
the real overall strength of the Armed Forces is about
90,000 people. All the rest is construction and other
organizations.

Of course, the former defense minister, whose son’s
wedding cost him the rank of colonel-general (now he is
regarded as lieutenant- general who refuses to turn up for
work), could not pass over in silence this unpleasant for
him fact at the press conference. He declared that “the
wedding caused no damage to the state”’. This is why he
declared his intention to ask Attorney General Vasil
Shaladonaw to relieve Vasil Dalhalew, chief of the
control service at the Presidential Administration, and
Viktar Sheyman, state secretary of the Security Council,
of their posts, and to initiate proceedings against them
for “fabrication of materials on the wedding”. Pavel
Kazlowski is going to press for holding parliamentary

Army. The consideration of P.Kazlowski’s civil libel
action of claim against President Lukashenka “with a

generally known, the former defense minister intends to
demand public apologies from the president. A sitting of
the court of the Sovietskiy district of Minsk, scheduled
for October, did not take place. The next sitting is
expected to be held in mid-December.

“‘However, the court and, perhaps, the Prosecutor’s
Office, are subject to pressure from the President's
Administration with a view to drag out the court exam-
ination by any means”, the former minister declared. He
is going to press for abolishing the President's decree No.
83, according to which he was dismissed. He has no
intention to cooperate with the present authorities, but
lays claim to a seat at the new parliament.

Army Officer Retention Seen as Difficult

MM2911123094 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA in
Russian 26 Nov 94 p 2

[Report by Valeriy Kovalev: “Is Trial by ‘Zaychik’
[Belarus currency] the Hardest?”’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] “Over the last two or three
months,” Consul Pavel Shestopalov of the Russian
Embassy in Belorussia [Belarus] said, “the number of
Belorussian servicemen asking us for citizenship has
risen dramatically. Some days around 40-50 officers
register at reception. Most of them are natives of Russia
who ended up in Belorussian Army ranks by the will of
fate following the breakup of the USSR.”

WESTERN REGION 57

Major General Aleksandr Zakharenko, chief of the
Belorussian Defense Ministry Cadre Directorate, noted
that this year 1,629 people have already been discharged.
Of them only 456, i.e. 28 percent, left the Army on
grounds of age and health, and 958 officers’ applications
for a discharge are currently being considered.

In 1993 2,390 Belorussian graduates were due to return
from military higher educational establishments in CIS
countries. But 407 new lieutenants stayed behind to
serve in the Russian Army, 208 were discharged at their
own request straight after graduating from school, and
268 after serving less than a year in the republic’s Armed
Forces. This year half of the graduates have already
chosen in favor of the Russian Armed Forces. Mean-
while there are now over 800 vacancies at platoon-
company level in the Belorussian Army. Take another
significant fact. Since early 1993 around 700 airmen
aged under 35 have left for Russia, and there are now
more aircraft in the Belorussian Air Force than pilots.
One of the main reasons for all this is the poor social
protection afforded to the military.

Belorussian officers, for instance, receive less money
than their Russian and Ukrainian counterparts by a
factor of 5.5 and 2.4 respectively.

As a result of all this the republic’s Armed Forces are
suffering increasingly marked losses of officer cadres
without a war, and this will take not only time but also
vast sums of money to replenish. The Belorussian lead-
ership finally seems to have realized that. By edict of
Belorussian President Alyaksandr Lukashenka, service-
men’s pay has been doubled as of | November. On the
orders of the head of state the Defense Ministry has set
up a working group tasked with drafting proposals to
considerably increase the social protection of people in
uniform.

Will the military department's propusals be accepted?
Belorussia’s highly complex economic position and its
extremely limited financial possibilities may regrettably
force Alyaksandr Lukashenka to cut the Defense Minis-
try’s hard-won program of measures to increase social
protection for servicemen.

Moldova

Cheptine on Need for Foreign Economic
Assistance

AU2911150994 Chisinau BASAPRESS in English 1930
GMT 28 Nov 94

[“‘Economic commentary”: “‘Moldova’s External
Debts”’]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Chisinau BASAPRESS, 28/
11/1994—In the years following the Declaration of Inde-
pendence, Moldova used foreign credits amounting to
$278.4 million. “The economic reform in Moldova is
impossible without foreign assistance,” declared Andrei

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

58 WESTERN REGION

Cheptine, vice minister of economics. On the other
hand, according to Cheptine, “most foreign credits were
offered in the form of goods Moldova needed during
various hard periods” and only 21.9 million were offered
in hard currency. Generally, the external borrowing was
required to maintain a favourable balance of payments
due to imports of critically needed goods. Deputy of the
Moldovan Agrarian Democratic Party Nicolae Oleinic,
former vice prime- minister, stated that “Moldova has
concluded 19 agreements according to which interna-
tional and European organizations and foreign states
granted credits worth $624.5 million, including $29.6
million for Dniester.” The former vice prime minister
also said that Moldova lacks a legal framework regarding
reimbursement and oversight over the use of credits,
while the National Bank, Ministry of Economics, and
Ministry of Finance failed to conduct an analysis to
identify economic motivations to secure credits or the
paramount directions for their use. At the parliamentary
debates on the draft budget, Minister of Finance Valeriu
Chitan said that at the beginning of 1995 Moldova's
external debt will amount to $398.9 million or 22
percent of the Gross National Product (GNP). Previ-
ously, Minister of Economics Valeriu Bobutac stated
that “the external debt in 1993 stood at $171 million for
which 5 million lei were paid as interest and in 1994 the
debt will amount to $390 million for which 55 million lei
will be paid as interest while in 1995 the interest due till
be 330 million lei.” According to experts, the stake on
intensification of exports and reimbursement of loans
from the revenues earned is illusory. The restrictive
monetary and credit policies promoted by the National
Bank and aimed at decreasing inflation have in fact
discouraged trade, particularly with the Western coun-
tries. The decrease in exports combined with other
negative evolutions like difficulties in forming the
budget and the impossibility to sell on the domestic
market the goods acquired by distributors at world prices
made it difficult to pay the interest within the terms set
and reimburse the credits. Andrei Cheptine, vice min-
ister of economics, stated that in 1994 “budget revenues
of 358.1 million lei stemming from the sale of goods
received as foreign credits were forecasted; But until
November | only 183 million lei were reimbursed.” In
this context, Gheorghe Rusu, head of the parliamentary
commission for finance and budget, pointed out that
“the government failed to ensure the reimbursement of
credits already starting this year” and that in the 1995
draft budget 60 million lei of foreign credits are not
covered with revenues. Deputy Nicolae Oleinic said that
“under the conditions of economic crisis, lack of a
program stipulating the expansion of production and use
of credits mainly for the current needs complicate the
reimbursement of credits.” Experts thir’ that Moldova
should ask for flexible conditions for reimbursement
of its foreign debts in order to the economic
recovery and promotion of a deve «.ent policy more
likely.

30 November 1994

Western Experts Help With Plant Conversions

AU2911150894 Chisinau BASAPRESS in English 1930
GMT 28 Nov 94

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Chisinau BASAPRESS, 28/
11/1994—Beginning December 1, 1994, a conversion
project for former military plants will be worked out in
association with experts from Belgium, France, and
Germany, BASA learned from Marian Lupu, of the
Economics Ministry. He stated that the European Union
allotted 1.5 million ECU through the “TACIS” [Tech-
nical Assistance to the Commonwealth of Independent
States] Program for the project. In May 1994 a tender
was announced and the firms with the most advanta-
geous offers will carry out the project for eight months.
Seveny-five percent of the whole amount of money were
allotted to the Belgian Company CECOFORMA, 20
percent to French “Thomson” and 5 percent to German
ESA. The Industry Ministry and six large former military
complex enterprises—Sigma, Semnal, Topaz, Mezon,
Micron and Raut—are the beneficiaries of this project.
After Moldova’s proclamation of independence, these
enterprises have substantially decreased their output due
to disrupted ties with the former Soviet military com-
plex. The enterprises will be switched to producing
equipment controlling aerial, terrestrial and maritime
traffic, electric appliances and other products.

AU2911160594 Chisinau BASAPRESS in English 1930
GMT 28 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Chisinau BASAPRESS, 28/
11/1994—Russian gas deliveries to Moldova constitu-
tion 9.3 million cubic metres daily, that is 65 percent of
the total needs of 14 million cubic metres, BASA learned
from Valeriu Miron, ““MOLDOVA-GAZ” deputy
director general. Earlier, Russian company “GAZ-
PROM” warned that gas deliveries will be reduced by 50
percent, but after the negotiations between Andrei Sang-
heli and “GAZPROM” representatives in Moscow, it
was agreed to resume initial deliveries. Miron mentioned
that population’s needs during winter increase 300-fold
in comparison with summer while gas prices are still
unchanged. Moldova’s debts for gas deliveries toward
Russia already exceed $200 million, Dniester accounting
for 50 percent of the debts. Miron noted that Moldova
exports agricultural products, in particular apples,
canned food, wines and cognac, to pay off its debts. In
the last two months Moldova exported products worth 7
million lei.

Chisinau, Iranians Discuss Commercial Exchange

Bilateral Ties Welcomed

LD3011111994 Tehran IRNA in English 1102 GMT
30 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, Nov. 30, IRNA—The
Moldovan parliament speaker, Pyotr Luchinskiy, said that
there was no obstacle in the way of cooperation with Iran
and welcomed expansion of bilateral ties. Luchinsky, on an
official visit to Russia, made the remark in reply to a
question by IRNA at a press conference.

mutual relations was promising because Moldova needed
Iran’s oil and at the same time was interested in presenting
its goods to the Iranian markets. The speaker noted that
exchange of different delegations between the two coun-

relations.

Kazakhstan

Agreement With India on $25 Million Credit
Grant

LD3011105394 Almaty Kazakh Radio Network in
Russian 0700 GMT 30 Nov 94

{[FBIS Translated Text] India has granted Kazakhstan a
credit worth $25 million. An agreement on this was
signed in Delhi by heads of the Trade and Industrial
Chamber of Kazakhstan and the Association of Trade
and Industrial Chambers of India.

The allocated funds will be first and foremost used for
financing the purchase of Indian goods, including phar-
maceutical products and equipment for the food
industry and agriculture. Apart from this India will
deliver to Kazakhstan huge batches of tea and products
from the light and leather industries.

The Kazakh Government has also set import quotas for
purchasing Indian copper, zinc, and mineral fertilizers.

As (Anil Agarval), the co-chairman of the joint Indian-
Kazakh commission on trade and economic coopera-
tion, reported, the agreement reached will help mutually
beneficial bilateral relations to expand considerably.

Foreign Minister: Relations With Russia Top
Priority

LD2811175194 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1601
GMT 28 Nov 94

[From the “Diplomatic Panorama” feature: by diplo-
matic correspondents Aleksandr Korzun, Igor Porshnev,
Yevgeniy Terekhov, and others]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Kazakhstan Foreign Minister
Kasymzhomat Tokayev said that developing relations
with Russia is the top priority in Kazakhstan's foreign
policy and will remain so “for many years, most likely
forever.”

Russia and Kazakhstan “are doomed to live together—
without giving any negative meaning to the word
‘doomed,”” Tokayev said in an exclusive interview with
Interfax in Alma-Ata [Almaty].

Asked about a visit to Kazakhstan by Russian President
Boris Yeltsin scheduled for December, the minister said:
“A final understanding on a Russo-Kazakh summit has
not yet been reached.” ““Work on this question is still
going on,” he added. He said he had recently sent a letter
to his Russian counterpart, Andrey Kozyrev, reaffirming
that the Kazakh side had nothing against the meeting
taking place “at the scheduled time.”

““(Kazakh) President Nursultan Nazarbayev will appar-
ently soon send a message to the Russian head of state,”
the Kazakh minister said.

He said Yeltsin’s “moderate reaction” to Nazarbayev’s
proposal for a new form of unification for post-Soviet
states—a Eurasian Union (EAU)—“‘does not and cannot
have any negative effect on the nature of bilateral
relations (between Kazakhstan and Russia).”

Tokayev voiced the hope that the proposal, which “con-
tains a considerable positive potential for the future,”
“‘will be used to develop the potential of the present-day
Commonwealth (of Independent States).”

Going over to October's CIS summit in Moscow,
Tokayev expressed the view that it “brought the EAU

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-230
30 November 1994

and CIS concepts into harmony with each other,” which

he saw as the meeting’s “principal achievement.”

He said there was a need for “a special document to
ensure peace, stability and security within the CIS.”

As for the problem of Kazakhstan’s Cossack community,
he described it as a “very sensitive” matter. “They (the
Cossacks) definitely have the right to develop their
distinctive culture and traditions. I’m aware that mili-
tary uniform and weapons are inseparable parts of
Cossack culture. But they have the same meaning for the
Chechens and Ingush. Does that mean that everybody
should be allowed to bear cold steel?” he said.

He condemned a Cossack rally in Alma-Ata on
November 19, which “contradicted the legislation and
was an insult to authority, which seeks stability in the
republic. And stability is part of international relations.”

“Political scientists believe that stability in a country as
large as Kazakhstan is a contribution to international
security. Those who try to undermine it should think
about what they are taking on themselves, in also in
international terms,” Tokayev concluded.

Activities of Semirechye Cossacks Suspended

LD3011101494 Almaty Kazakh Radio Network in
Russian 1500 GMT 29 Nov 94

[BIS Translated Text] The activities of the Semirechye
Cossacks have been suspended. As KAZTAG has
reported, Nagashbay Shaykenov, justice minister of
Kazakhstan, has issued an order suspending the activi-
ties of the Society for Assistance to the Semirechye
Cossacks.

The order says that on | June this year the Ministry of
Justice registered the society’s statutes, which did not
contain any contradictions to the Constitution and
existing Kazakh legislation. According to the registration
documents the society is not a military formation, does
not pursue any political goals, and does not advocate
ethnic, social, or religious intolerance or class discrimi-
nation. Participation in the concept proposed by the
President Nursultan Nazarbayev on the ideological con-
solidation of society, the strengthening of civic accord,
and the harmonization of interethnic relations and assis-
tance in reviving the Cossacks’ progressive historic tra-
ditions, customs, and rites were proclaimed as the soci-
ety’s main aims. ;

As the order of the Ministry of Justice notes, however,
the society started its practical activities by breaking the
law. Only a month and a half aftzr registration the
Ministry of Justice had to warn th« society that it was
violating clauses of the Constituiion. Later it was repeat-
edly explained verbally to the leaders of this formation
that such violations were inadmissible.

However, as is clear from reports from the prosecution
bodies, the society, in contradiction of Article 55 of the
republic’s Constitution, has been acting as a structural

CENTRAL ASIA 61

division of the Russian Cossack community, which has
been elevated to the rank of an armed force. In violation
of Article 64 of the Constitution and the law on the
administrative structure of the Republic of Kazakhstan,
the society does not recognize the existing administra-
tive and territorial divisions in the republic and has
decided on names for settlements without authorization.

The order stresses that illegal actions of an everyday
nature aimed at whipping up interethnic conflicts were
committed and unauthorized meetings, calling for things
that were not in line with the society’s purposes and goals
established in the statutes, were held by some divisions
and members of the society in the Alma-Ata [Almaty]
and Taldy-Kurgan regions and in Alma-Ata.

Acting in accordance with Article 24 of the law on public
organizations in the Kazakh Soviet Socialist Republic,
the justice minister has issued an order suspending the
activities of the Society for Assistance to the Semirechye
Cossacks and its President Ovsyannikov, for six months
from 28 November 1994 until 28 May 1995 for violating
Articles 55 and 64 of the Constitution, Articles 4 and 17
of the law on public organizations in the Kazakh Soviet
Socialist Republic, Article 7 of the law on universal
military duties and military service, and the regulations
of the society’s own statutes.
